Greek (Kühn)

εὔδηλον δ’ ὅτι πρώτας μὲν ὀνομάζω θερμότητα καὶ ψυχρότητα καὶ ξηρότητα 764 καὶ ὑγρότητα καὶ τὰς ἐκ τούτων συνθέτους κατὰ συζυγίαν ἡντιναοῦν. δευτέρας δὲ τὰς ἑπομένας αὐταῖς, ὑπὲρ ὧν ἐπὶ πλέον ἐν τῷ πέμπτῳ τῆς τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων πραγματείας διῆλθον, ἐπιδεικνὺς ἔνια μὲν τῶν φαρμάκων οὐ μόνων τῶν συνθέτων, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν αὐτοφυῶν μαλάττειν, ἔνια δὲ σκληρύνειν, ὥσπερ καὶ τὰ μὲν ἐμπλάττειν, τὰ δὲ ἐκφράττειν, καὶ τὰ μὲν ἐπιτρέφειν ῥύπον, τὰ δὲ καὶ τὸν ἐπικείμενον ἀποῤῥύπτειν, καὶ τὰ μὲν λεπτύνειν, τὰ δὲ παχύνειν, καὶ τὰ μὲν πυκνοῦν, τὰ δὲ ἀραιοῦν, καὶ τὰ μὲν ἀνατρέφειν σάρκα, τὰ δὲ ἀνακαθαίρειν, καὶ τὰ μὲν ἐπουλοῦν, τὰ δὲ ἑλκοῦν, καὶ τὰ μὲν ἕλκειν, τὰ δὲ ἀποκρούεσθαι, ἑτέρας τε τοιαύτας ἐν αὐτοῖς διαφοράς. εἴρηται δέ μοι καὶ διὰ τῶν ἔμπροσθεν ὑπομνημάτων ἥτις τῶν ἐναντίων ταῖς δυνάμεσι φαρμάκων ἀναγκαία μίξις, ἧς ἀναμνήσω καὶ νῦν ὀλίγον ὕστερον. ἄμεινον γάρ μοι δοκεῖ γράψαι τινὰ δύο καὶ τρία φάρμακα τῶν ἐνδόξων πολυχρήστων, εἶτ’ ἐξηγήσασθαι τὸν λόγον τῆς συνθέσεως αὐτῶν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐμπλάττειν, ἐκφράττειν

It is quite evident that by 'primary' I mean heat and cold and dryness 764 and moisture, and those compounded from these according to any conjugation whatsoever; and by 'secondary' those that follow upon these, about which I went through at greater length in the fifth book of the treatise on simple drugs, showing that some drugs, not only of the compound ones but also of the naturally occurring ones, soften, while others harden, just as also some plug up and others unblock, and some breed filth upon the part while others cleanse away even the filth already lying upon it, and some thin, others thicken, and some condense, others rarefy, and some regenerate flesh, others cleanse it away, and some cicatrize, others ulcerate, and some draw, others repel, and other such differences among them. It has also been stated by me in the preceding commentaries what the necessary mixture of drugs opposite in their powers is, of which I shall remind you now too a little later. For it seems better to me to write out some two or three drugs among the renowned multi-purpose ones, and then to explain the rationale of their composition.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μόνων, ἐμπλάττειν, ἐπιτρέφειν ῥύπον, ἀνακαθαίρειν

It is quite clear that by 'primary' I mean heat and cold and dryness 764 and moisture, together with the qualities compounded from these in any pairing whatever; and by 'secondary' I mean those that follow upon them. I discussed these at greater length in the fifth book of my treatise on simple drugs, where I showed that some drugs, not only among the compound ones but also among the natural ones, soften, while others harden. In the same way some block up the passages and others clear obstructions; some generate dirt in addition, while others even cleanse away the dirt lying on the surface; some make thin and others thicken; some condense and others rarefy; some regenerate flesh and others clean it away; some cicatrize and others ulcerate; some draw and others repel; and there are other such differences among them. I have also stated in the earlier treatises what the necessary mixing of drugs contrary in their powers is, and I shall recall it now as well, a little later. For it seems better to me to write out some two or three of the renowned polychrest drugs, and then to explain the rationale of their composition.

Greek (Kühn)

ἄρξομαι δὲ ἀπὸ τῶν ἅπασι γνωρίμων, ἃ κατά τε τὸν Ἥραν νάρθηκα γέγραπται καὶ παρ’ 765 ἄλλοις τῶν φαρμακίτιδας βίβλους συνθέντων. ἐπεὶ δὲ κατὰ τὸ πρὸ τούτου βιβλίον ὁ λόγος ἐγένετό μοι περὶ τῶν δυσεπούλωτα θεραπευουσῶν ἕλκη δυνάμεων, ἔδοξέ μοι καὶ νῦν ἐκείνας τῶν πολυχρήστων ἐμπλάστρων ἐξηγήσασθαι πρώτας, ὅσαι κακοήθων ἕνεκα συνετέθησαν, ἃ καλοῦσιν ἔνιοι χειρώνεια ὅσαι δ’ ἄλλαι πολύχρηστοι καλοῦνται διὰ τὸ πρὸς πολλὰ μὲν ἁρμόττειν ἕτερα πάθη, μὴ μέντοι καὶ πρὸς τὰ χειρώνεια, ταύτας αὖθις ἐφεξῆς ἀλλήλαις διελθεῖν ἔγνωκα. [Περὶ τῶν ὑπὸ Ἥρα γεγραμμένων.] Γεγραμμένης οὖν ἐν τῇ βίβλῳ τῶν φαρμάκων ὑπὸ τοῦ Καππάδοκος Ἥρα, πρὸς τὰ τοιαῦτα τῶν ἑλκῶν ἐμπλάστρου τινὸς ἄκρως ποιούσης, ἀπ’ αὐτῆς ἄρξομαι τὴν λέξιν παραγράψας τἀνδρὸς ᾧδέ πως ἔχουσαν. εὔχρους ἔναιμος, ἐπουλωτικὴ, κολλῶσα μὲν ἀκραιφνῶς, πληροῦσα δὲ καὶ ἐπουλοῦσα, ἐκλυθεῖσα μετὰ κηρωτῆς καὶ ἔτι μᾶλλον περὶ τὴν ἐπούλωσιν. ποιεῖ μέντοι καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν χειρωνείων ἑλκῶν καὶ τῶν ἄλλων τῶν παραπλησίων ἄκρως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φαρμακίτιδας, εὔχρους, ἔναιμος, ἀκραιφνῶς

I shall begin from those known to all, which have been recorded both in the Narthex of Heras and among 765 others who have compiled books of drugs (pharmakitides). And since in the book before this one my discourse concerned the compound remedies (dynameis) that treat ulcers hard to cicatrize, it seemed good to me now too to expound first those of the plasters of many uses (polychrēsta) which were composed for the sake of malignant ulcers, which some call cheironeia; and as for the others that are called polychrēsta because they are suitable for many other affections, though not, however, also for the cheironeia, these I have resolved to go through afterwards, one after another. [On those written by Heras.] Since, then, a certain plaster that works excellently against such ulcers has been recorded in the book of drugs by Heras the Cappadocian, I shall begin from it, transcribing the man's wording, which runs roughly as follows. A good-coloured (euchrous) plaster for bloody wounds (enaimos), cicatrizing, agglutinating perfectly (akraiphnōs), and also filling up and cicatrizing, and, when dissolved with cerate, still more so with regard to cicatrization. It also works excellently, however, on the cheironeian ulcers and the others similar to them.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἔναιμος, ἀκραιφνῶς, χειρώνεια, ἔτι μᾶλλον περὶ τὴν ἐπούλωσιν, φαρμακίτιδας

I shall begin from those known to all, which are written in the Narthex of Heras and in 765 others of those who have composed books of drugs. And since in the book before this my discussion concerned the powers that treat hard-to-cicatrise ulcers, it seemed good to me now too to explain first those of the polychrest plasters that were compounded for the sake of malignant ulcers, which some call Chironian; and as for the other plasters that are called polychrest because they suit many other affections, though not the Chironian ones as well, these I have decided to go through afterwards, one after another. [On those written by Heras.] Since, then, in the book of drugs by Heras the Cappadocian there is written a plaster that works supremely well on ulcers of this kind, I shall begin from it, setting down the man's text, which runs roughly thus: 'Of good colour, enaemon, cicatrizing; agglutinating when used unmixed, but filling up and cicatrizing when dissolved with cerate, and still more so for cicatrization. It also works supremely well on Chironian ulcers and on others similar to them.'

Greek (Kühn)

τούς τε γὰρ τύλους τήκει καὶ 766 μετὰ ταῦτα γνησίαν σάρκα γεννᾷ. λίαν λευκή. ♃ κηροῦ λίτρας β΄. φρυκτῆς λίτρας γ΄. ἰοῦ ξυστοῦ γο στ΄. μάννης γο γ΄. ἐλαίου κοτύλας ε΄. ὄξους ξε. α΄. τὰ τηκτὰ κατὰ τῶν ξηρῶν χρῶ τῇ ἐμπλάστρῳ πρὸς τραύματα καὶ ἀνιεμένη δὲ σὺν ῥοδίνῳ πυοποιεῖ καὶ ἀνακαθαίρει τῇ κηρωτῇ διεκλυθείσῃ εἰς ἐπούλωσιν. τινές γε μὴν τῆς ῥητίνης ἔβαλον φρυκτῆς λίτρας β΄. καὶ τερμινθίνης λίτρας δύο, καὶ οὕτω παρασκευάσαντες ἀνελάμβανον αὐτὴν ἰῷ ξυστῷ, ὡς κολλυρίου τόνον ἔχειν, καὶ τοῦτο ἐνετίθεσαν ὅπου τύλους ἐκτῆξαι ἤθελον. εὐχερῶς γὰρ τοῦτο ποιεῖ, ὡς καὶ ὀστᾶ ἀφιστάναι. καὶ ταύτην μὲν οὖν εἰκότως ἐπαινεῖ τὴν ἔμπλαστρον ὁ Ἥρας, ὡς ἐκ τοῦ πλήθους τοῦ ἰοῦ καὶ τοῦ ὄξους τὸ δραστήριον ἔχουσαν, εἴ τι μεμνήμεθα τῶν εἰρημένων κατὰ τὸ πρὸ τούτου βιβλίον. ἐπαινεῖ δὲ καὶ τὴν ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ καλουμένην πανάκειαν, ἣν ἐφεξῆς γράφω κατὰ τὴν ἐκείνου λέξιν. πανάκεια Ἥρα ἡ ὑπό τινων ὑγεία λεγομένη. πρὸς πᾶσαν νομὴν καὶ κακοήθειαν. ἵστησι δὲ τὰς νομὰς οὔτ’ ἐσχάρας ποιοῦσα οὔτε σκληρύνουσα τὰ πέριξ σώματα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῇ κηρωτῇ διεκλυθείσῃ, τὰ τηκτὰ κατὰ τῶν ξηρῶν

For it melts away the calluses and 766 after this generates genuine flesh. Very white. Take: wax, 2 litrai; roasted (phrykte) resin, 3 litrai; scraped verdigris, 6 ounces; manna, 3 ounces; oil, 5 kotylai; vinegar, 1 xestes. The melted ingredients are poured over the dry ones. Use the plaster for wounds; and when it is slackened with rose-oil it produces pus and cleanses; when dissolved with the cerate, for cicatrization. Some, however, put in, of the resin, 2 litrai of roasted (phrykte) and two litrai of terebinth, and having prepared it thus they took it up with scraped verdigris so that it had the firmness of a collyrium, and they inserted this wherever they wished to melt away calluses. For it does this readily, so as even to detach bones. And so Heras reasonably praises this plaster, as having its active power from the quantity of the verdigris and the vinegar, if we remember at all what was said in the book before this one. He also praises the one called by him panacea, which I write out next in his own words. The panacea of Heras, called by some 'Health' (hygieia). For every spreading sore (nome) and malignancy. It checks the spreading sores, neither producing eschars nor hardening the surrounding bodies.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ♃, γο, ξε., ἀνιεμένη, τὰ τηκτὰ κατὰ τῶν ξηρῶν, τόνον, νομὴν

For it melts down callosities, and 766 after this it generates genuine flesh. Very white. ♃ [Take]: wax, 2 litrai; roasted resin, 3 litrai; scraped verdigris, 6 ounces; manna, 3 ounces; oil, 5 kotylai; vinegar, 1 xestes. The meltable ingredients over the dry. Use the plaster for wounds; and when it is let down with rose oil it produces pus and cleans out; dissolved with cerate, for cicatrization. Some, however, put in, of the resin, 2 litrai of the roasted and two litrai of the terebinth. Having prepared it in this way they took it up with scraped verdigris, so that it had the consistency of a collyrium, and they inserted this wherever they wished to melt away callosities. For it does this easily, so as even to detach bones. This plaster, then, Heras reasonably praises, since it has its efficacy from the large quantity of the verdigris and the vinegar, if we remember anything of what was said in the book before this one. He also praises the one he calls panacea, which I write out next in his own wording. Panacea of Heras, called by some Hygieia. For every spreading ulceration and malignancy. It halts spreading ulcerations, neither producing eschars nor hardening the surrounding bodies.

Greek (Kühn)

ῥηγνύουσα δὲ καὶ μαραίνουσα πᾶν 767 τὸ διεφθαρμένον, ὡς μηδὲν διαφέρειν ὑγρᾶς σταφίδος. ἐπέχει δὲ καὶ φορὰν αἵματος τήν τε ἄλλην καὶ τὴν δι’ αἱμοῤῥοΐδων διειμένη μυρσίνῳ καὶ τὰς αἱμοῤῥοΐδας αὐτὰς μαραίνει καὶ δαπανᾷ. πρὸς τούτοις δὲ καὶ σύριγγας ἰᾶται καὶ τοὺς ὀνομαζομένους ἄνθρακας καὶ τοὺς αἰγίλωπας καὶ τὰ χειρώνεια λεγόμενα καὶ καθόλου τὸ τῶν δυσιάτων καὶ χρονίων γένος, εἰς μὲν τοὺς ἀφανεῖς τόπους οἷον ὑστέρας διατηκομένη μύρῳ κυπρίνῳ, τοῖς δ’ ἔξωθεν ἄμεινόν ἐστιν, ὡς ἑκάστην τῶν ἄλλων οὕτως ἐπιτιθέναι. παντὶ δὲ ἕλκει ἐπιτεθεῖσα, κᾂν ᾖ τῶν ἀγρίων, πραΰτερον εὐθὺς ποιήσει καὶ τοὺς πόνους ἠπιωτέρους καὶ τὰς ἀγρυπνίας. ἡ δὲ δύναμίς ἐστιν αὕτη. χαμαιπίτυος δραχ. δ΄. συμφύτου δραχ. δ΄. χαμαιλέοντος δραχ. δ΄. πρασίου δραχ. δ΄. οἱ δὲ στ΄. πολίου δραχ. στ΄. κενταυρίου λεπτοῦ δραχ. στ΄. ἑλενίου δραχ. δ΄. ἀριστολοχίας δραχ. δ΄. λιβάνου δραχ. ιβ΄ S". σμύρνης δραχ. γ΄. ἀλόης δραχ. στ΄. χαλβάνης δραχ. στ΄. ὀβολὸς εἷς S". ἐλαίου δραχ. ρπ΄. μέλιτος δραχ. λ΄. προπόλεως δραχ. β΄. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑγρᾶς σταφίδος, χειρώνεια

but breaking up and withering away everything that has been corrupted, so that it differs in no way from a moist raisin. It also checks a flow of blood, both the other kinds and that through haemorrhoids, when dissolved in myrtle oil, and it withers and consumes the haemorrhoids themselves. In addition to these, it also heals fistulas and the so-called carbuncles (anthrakes) and the aegilopes and the so-called Chironian ulcers, and in general the whole class of hard-to-heal and chronic conditions—for the hidden places, such as the womb, being melted down in henna oil, while for the external ones it is better to apply it in the same way as each of the others. Applied to any ulcer, even if it is one of the wild (malignant) kind, it will at once make it milder and render the pains and the sleeplessness gentler. The formula is this: ground-pine (chamaepitys) 4 drachms; comfrey 4 drachms; chamaeleon 4 drachms; horehound 4 drachms (others say 6); polion 6 drachms; small centaury 6 drachms; elecampane 4 drachms; aristolochia 4 drachms; frankincense 12½ drachms; myrrh 3 drachms; aloe 6 drachms; galbanum 6 drachms and one and a half obols; oil 180 drachms; honey 30 drachms; propolis 2½ drachms; split alum 6 drachms and one and a half obols;

but breaking up and withering all 767 that is corrupted, so that it differs in no way from a moist raisin. When dissolved with myrtle oil it also checks a flux of blood, both the other kind and that through haemorrhoids, and it withers and consumes the haemorrhoids themselves. In addition to these it also heals fistulae, the so-called carbuncles (anthrakes), the aigilopes, the so-called Chironian [ulcers], and in general the whole class of hard-to-heal and chronic [ulcers]. For the hidden places, such as the uterus, it is melted down with unguent of cypros; for the external ones it is better to apply it in the same way as each of the other [plasters]. of terebinth 768 6 drachms, one and a half obols; of chalcitis 6 drachms, one and a half obols; of asphalt 50 drachms; of ampelitis earth 50 drachms; of litharge 50 drachms; of oak-gall 3 drachms; of fine powder of iris 3 drachms. One must pound the herbs and the roots on the preceding day and put them into the oil. When they have remained in it through the whole night, in the morning one must add the litharge, the ampelitis and the asphalt and boil over a gentle fire. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But we must note that the material itself differs from that of the drugs described in the book before this one for the hard-to-treat 769 ulcers. For those were composed of mineral substances alone, some of few, some of more, with a few among them containing roots or herbs. But with this panacea the opposite is the case: it takes both chamaeleon and centaury, each of which is sufficient to benefit malignant ulcers. It takes also aristolochia and iris, weaker indeed than centaury and chamaeleon with respect to the cure of malignant ulcers, yet not to be rejected. And it takes strong herbs, which are often able even on their own to treat malignant ulcers: chamaepitys, polion and comfrey. Stronger than these are horehound and elecampane. Horehound, then, one might use even alone in cases where the flesh in the ulcer is quite moist and rather foul; but elecampane is not suitable, when sprinkled on alone, even in these cases, just as no other acrid substance is either.

For you learned, in the treatise on simple drugs, the difference between acrid and bitter drugs: the acrid ones being irritant and biting and hot and bringing fluxes upon the affected parts, 770 while the bitter ones are drying without biting and heating, and for this reason suitable for ulcerated parts. But since the man who compounded these herbs had set himself to make from them a plaster, not a dry dusting-powder, and for the composition of the plaster he was going to mix in either wax or resin, or at any rate litharge boiled for a longer time, by which the power of the herbs was being weakened, he mixed with them some of the drugs that are so strong that they cannot be applied to ulcers on their own. These strong drugs have a twofold general power and quality: some an austere and harsh one, others an acrid and harsh one. For you learned in the treatise on simple drugs the difference between acrid and bitter drugs: the acrid ones are irritant, biting and hot, and cause a flux into the affected parts 770; the bitter ones are drying without biting and heating, and for this reason are suitable for ulcerated parts. Chalcitis has both qualities strongly, as also the potencies that go with them, being both acrid and astringent.

It has been said in what precedes that the austere quality is necessary for malignant ulcers, and especially 771 when they are rheumatic. To all these, then, which I have mentioned, litharge and resin and frankincense were mixed in, in order that the drug might become plaster-like. But litharge belongs to the intermediate material, while frankincense is concoctive and pus-producing, for those who take the well-tempered nature as the reference. For it was shown that for moister bodies it is flesh-producing, and moreover that of the concoctive drugs, which indeed also possess the so-called suppurative power, none is strongly discutient, just as none is acrid either. For such things heat moderately, and moreover they soften rather than dry. Rightly, then, some frankincense too was mixed into the composition of the plaster before us, so that it might have something soothing and mild as well. And the oil too is evidently of the same power, mixed in, according to the primary reason, for the sake of the boiling of the plaster, but carrying along with it two powers, the soothing one and the one that blunts the strong drugs, whether these be of an acrid or of an austere power.

It has been said in what precedes that the austere quality is necessary for malignant ulcers, and especially 771 when they are fluxional. With all these, then, that I have mentioned were mixed litharge, resin and frankincense, so that the drug might become plaster-like. But litharge belongs to the intermediate material, while frankincense is concoctive and pus-producing, if we look to the well-tempered nature. For it was shown that in moister bodies it is flesh-producing. For such drugs heat moderately, and moreover soften rather than dry. It was therefore well that some frankincense too was mixed in the composition of the plaster under discussion, so that it might also have something soothing and mild. Clearly the oil too is of the same power. In the first instance it was mixed in for the sake of boiling the plaster, but it brings with it two powers in itself: the soothing power, and the power that blunts strong drugs, whether these are of acrid or of austere power.

Greek (Kühn)

ἡ δὲ ἀμπελῖτις γῆ ξηραίνει τε καὶ διαφορεῖ 772 μηδὲν ἔχουσα δριμὺ σαφῶς. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τοῖς κακοήθεσιν ἕλκεσιν ἐπιτήδειός ἐστιν, εἰ καὶ μόνην αὐτὴν λειώσας ἐπιπάττοις. ἡ χαλβάνη δὲ καὶ αὐτὴ τῷ πεπτική τε εἶναι καὶ μαλακτικὴ καὶ παρηγορική τε καὶ ἀνώδυνος ἐμίχθη κατὰ τὴν προκειμένην σύνθεσιν, ἔχοντος μὲν καὶ τοῦ λιβανωτοῦ τὰς αὐτὰς δυνάμεις, ἀλλὰ ἀσθενεστέρας ἢ κατὰ τὴν χαλβάνην, ἐκείνης γε κατ’ ἀμφοτέρας ἰσχυροτέρας ὑπαρχούσης. ἡ τερμινθίνη δὲ ῥητίνη καὶ αὐτὴ μετὰ τοῦ ῥυπτικοῦ τε καὶ διαφορητικοῦ πεπτικὸν ἔχει τι τῶν τοιούτων διαθέσεων. ἡ δὲ ἄσφαλτος ἴσμεν ὅτι ξηραντικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως ἅμα θερμασίᾳ μὴ πάνυ σφοδρᾷ. τούτοις οὖν ἅπασιν ὁ συνθεὶς τὴν ἔμπλαστρον ἔμιξεν ἑλκτικῆς δυνάμεως φάρμακον τὴν πρόπολιν, ἐπειδὴ πολύχρηστον ὑπάρχειν αὐτὴν ἐβούλετο. πάντα δ’ ὅσα πολύχρηστα ποικιλωτάτας ἀναγκαῖον ἔχειν δυνάμεις, ὅπως θεραπεύῃ διαθέσεις πλείονας. οὕτως δὲ καὶ τοῦ μέλιτος ἔμιξεν οὐκ ὀλίγον, εἰδὼς αὐτὸ ξηραῖνόν τε καὶ διαφοροῦν οἰκειότατόν τε τοῖς σηπομένοις ὑπάρχον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Ampelitis earth both dries and disperses, 772 having nothing clearly acrid. And for this reason it is suitable for malignant ulcers, even if you should triturate it by itself and sprinkle it on. Galbanum too was itself mixed into the present composition because it is concoctive and emollient and soothing and anodyne, frankincense also having the same powers, but weaker than in the case of galbanum, since the latter is stronger in respect of both. Terebinth resin too, along with its detergent and discutient quality, has something concoctive of such conditions. As for bitumen, we know that it is of a drying power together with a heat that is not very vehement. To all these, then, the one who composed the plaster mixed in a drug of attractive power, propolis, since he wished it to be a polychreston (of many uses). And all drugs that are of many uses must necessarily have the most varied powers, so that they may treat a greater number of conditions. So too he mixed in no small amount of honey, knowing that it dries and disperses and is most appropriate for parts that are putrefying.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For it too, in turn, belongs among the things that do not putrefy, and it keeps the things mixed with it such, 773 except for those that are in the highest degree disposed to putrefaction, which are both moist and hot. I do not know what it was that led Heras to decide to write this panacea with smaller proportions, when it has been written by others with larger ones. For some wrote it as having everything doubled, and some quadrupled.

[The Isis, called by some that of Epigonus.] There is a drug of many uses (polychrēston), which they call Isis, praised by all. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑπόῤῥυσιν, εἰς ὑπόῤῥυσιν δ’ ὄντων ἡμῶν, ὑποφορὰς

For it forms callus more quickly and more safely over all recent fistulas, old sinuses (ὑποφοραί), hollows (κόλποι), phymata, parotid swellings, and the abscesses in the breasts and 775 armpits, either so that they do not arise or, once they have arisen, are cured. And when we are in a state of defluxion (ὑπόρρυσις), it further removes scrofulous swellings together with their coats, bringing them to suppuration very quickly; it also stops a flux of the eyes when applied to the forehead. It also takes away livid patches, circumscribes gangrenes, cleanses putrefactions, and, in a word, it is good for everything. Take: wax, 100 drachmas; terebinth, 200 drachmas, though others say 100; scale of red copper, 8 drachmas; scraped verdigris, 8 drachmas; round aristolochia, 8 drachmas; frankincense, 8 drachmas; ammoniac salt, 8 drachmas; ammoniac incense, 8 drachmas; burnt alum or round alum, 6 drachmas; burnt copper, 8 drachmas; myrrh, 12 drachmas; aloe, 12 drachmas; galbanum, 12 drachmas; old oil, 1 kotyle; acrid vinegar, 2 kotylai. Pour the meltable ingredients into a mortar over the dry ones. Thus Heras wrote the Isis, without further adding to it the whole of its use in particular cases, as he was accustomed to do with others.

καὶ μέντοι κᾀν τῷ καλεῖν αὐτὴν Ἐπιγόνου διαφέρεται πολλοῖς, οὓς ἀκριβέστερον ἡγοῦμαι περὶ τοῦ Ἐπιγόνου γεγραφέναι τά τε 776 ἄλλα καὶ ὡς ἐν εἱρκτῇ κατακεκλεισμένος ἰασάμενός τέ τινα τῶν πολὺ δυναμένων ἀφείθη διὰ τοῦτο. προστεθεικέναι δ’ αὐτόν φασι τὴν τοῦ δρακοντίου ῥίζαν τῷ προγεγραμμένῳ φαρμάκῳ, κᾀγὼ διὰ παντὸς οὕτως ἐχρησάμην. ἐπειδὴ καὶ τῶν διδασκάλων μού τις ἐχρῆτο, βαλὼν εἰς τὴν γεγραμμένην συμμετρίαν ὑπὸ τοῦ Ἥρᾳ δραχ. η΄. τοῦ δρακοντίου. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τοῦ ἐλαίου μὲν ἐνέβαλεν ἥμισυ κυάθου, ὄξους δὲ κο. α΄, ἔβαλλε δὲ καὶ τὴν κολοφωνίαν ῥητίνην, οὐχ ὥσπερ ὅ Ἥρας τὴν τερμινθίνην, ἀλλὰ κᾀν τῇ κατασκευῇ τῆς ἐμπλάστρου διεφώνει τῷ Ἥρᾳ, τὴν χαλβάνην τῷ ὄξει συγκατεργαζόμενος τοῖς ἄλλοις ἐν θυείᾳ. καὶ οὕτως ἀναμιγνὺς αὐτὰ, καθάπερ καὶ ὁ Ἥρας φησὶ, τοῖς τηκτοῖς ἐμάλασσέ τε μετὰ ταῦτα τὸ φάρμακον, ἐπὶ πλεῖστον ἐν ταῖς χερσὶ προσαπτόμενος ὄξους δριμυτάτου καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα εἰς χαλκοῦν ἀγγεῖον κατετίθετο καὶ καταχύσας ἐφύλλαττε χρόνῳ πλείονι καὶ οὕτως ἀνελόμενος ἐχρῆτο. προσηγόρευε δὲ αὐτὴν, ὡς καὶ ἄλλοι τινὲς, Ἕρμωνος τοῦ ἱερογραμματέως. ἐν ἑτέροις δ’ ἂν εὕροις γεγραμμένην ἐκ τῶν ἀδύτων εἶναι καὶ τήνδε κατὰ τὸ Ἡφαίστειον ἐν Αἰγύπτῳ, καθάπερ καὶ τὴν διὰ τοῦ δικτάμνου.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προσαπτόμενος ὄξους δριμυτάτου, καταχύσας

And indeed, also in calling it the plaster "of Epigonus" he differs from many, who I consider to have written more accurately about Epigonus, both on the other 776 matters and that, having been shut up in prison and having cured one of the very powerful men, he was released on that account. They say that he added the root of dracontium to the drug written above, and I myself have always used it in this way, since one of my teachers too used it, putting into the proportion written by Heras 8 drachmas of dracontium. Moreover, he put in half a cyathus of oil and 1 cotyle of vinegar, and he also put in colophony resin, not, as Heras did, terebinth; but also in the preparation of the plaster he disagreed with Heras, working up the galbanum with the vinegar together with the other ingredients in a mortar. And mixing them in this way, as Heras too says, with the meltable ingredients, he then kneaded the drug, for a very long time in his hands, applying very sharp vinegar; and after this he deposited it in a bronze vessel and, having poured [vinegar] over it, kept it for a longer time, and then, taking it up, used it. He called it, as some others also do, the plaster of Hermon the sacred scribe. In others you would find it written that this one too is from the inner sanctuaries at the Hephaesteum in Egypt, just like the one made with dittany.

Moreover, in calling it the plaster of Epigonus he also disagrees with many, who, I believe, have written more accurately about Epigonus, both on other matters and on how, having been shut up in prison 776 and having healed one of the very powerful men, he was released on that account. In other authors you may find it written that this one too comes from the inner sanctuaries at the temple of Hephaestus in Egypt, just like the plaster with dittany.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

777 As for the proportion of the simple drugs that compose it, however, nearly all have written it the same, so that even if the heading differs, you should not suppose that the sacred plaster from the inner sanctuaries, that of Hermon the sacred scribe, and that of Epigonus are each a different one; for it is called by all these names. Melted together with a moist cerate it cleanses and cicatrises ulcers. And some have written that it is to be diluted with three times its quantity of cerate, others with twice, each party having fixed the proportion without making any distinction. For in those cases in which it renders the ulcer sufficiently clean if mixed with three parts of cerate, one must not mix in the double; but in those cases in which it cleanses less adequately, then the double quantity of cerate must be employed. For we know that every drug of this kind is more active when not mixed with much cerate, and weaker when mixed with more.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

It is no worse, however, to say at least this much about dracontium too: that in summer, when the stem and the whole plant have dried up, one should take up the root, then, having placed it in a room that is dry but does not receive the sun, a little later, after six or seven days, cut the root into 778 small disks, and having threaded these on a linen thread hang them again in a room that is dry but sunless, and so, whenever you wish to prepare the drug, pound the root. This root, even by itself, when pounded and well triturated so as to become dust-fine, is curative of malignant ulcers. [The plaster made with dittany.] The plaster prepared with dittany too they call hiera, saying that this prescription also was found in the temple of Hephaestus at Memphis. And indeed all mention it with proportions of this kind or ones differing only slightly. Concerning this one too it will suffice to append the wording of Heras, which runs as follows: Hiera, the one from the temple of Hephaestus at Memphis.

It is not amiss also to say this much about dracontium: that in summer, when the stalk and the whole plant have dried, one must take up the root; then, having put it in a house that is dry but gets no sun, a little later, after six or seven days, one must cut the root into 778 little discs, and, threading these on a linen thread, hang them up again in a house that is dry but sunless; and so, whenever you wish to prepare the drug, pound the root. The root by itself, too, pounded and ground smooth well enough to become fine as down, is curative of malignant ulcers. And all of them record it with these same proportions, or with proportions differing a little. Concerning this one too it will suffice to copy out the text of Heras, which runs as follows: 'Sacred plaster, the one from the temple of Hephaestus at Memphis.'

It is effective for fresh bleeding wounds, for rheumatic ones, for severed sinews, old ulcers, undermining sores, contusions, putrefactions, carbuncles, blackenings, rheumatic ulcers, those hard to cicatrize, parotid swellings, chilblains, abscesses, scrofulous swellings, boils, indurations; it draws out missiles, splinters, bones from the head; wounds of wild beasts and bites of scorpions, of phalangia, 779 of the shrew-mouse, human bites. For every pain of the neck, shoulder and joints, in place of a malagma. It checks fluxes of the eyes, not only recent but also old ones, and it is effective for headache, smeared on when dissolved in rose oil; and also for pain of the womb, both anointed on and used as a fumigation from below. The plaster, when burnt as a fumigant, also drives away venomous creatures. It consists of: old oil, 2 cotylae; litharge, 100 drachms; scraped verdigris, 8 drachms; scale of copper, 6 drachms; liquid Colophonian resin, or according to some pine resin, 50 drachms; manna of frankincense, 12 drachms; ammoniac incense, 16 drachms; burnt copper, 8 drachms; diphryges, 6 drachms; gentian, 6 drachms; aloe, 8 drachms; galbanum, 12 drachms; propolis, 8 drachms; wax, 25 drachms; aristolochia, 10 drachms; Cretan dittany, 10 drachms.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μελανίας, ἀνειμένη, μάννης, λεπίδος, ὑπόνομα

Greek (Kühn)

ἕψε ἐν λοπάδι μεγάλῃ ἐπὶ ἀνθράκων λιθάργυρον, ἔλαιον ὡς ἀμόλυντον κατὰ πᾶν εἶναι ἢ παρὰ μικρὸν, εἶτα ἄρας τὴν χύτραν, μίσγε ἰὸν καὶ λεπίδα καὶ ἕψε καὶ μὴ μολυνέτω κατὰ πᾶν, εἶτα πάλιν βαστάσας βάλε ῥητίνην καὶ ἀμμωνιακὸν σεσησμένα, κινῶν ἕως παύσηται ζέων, εἶτα ἐπιθεὶς καὶ μικρὸν ἐάσας βαστάσας ἔμπασσε χαλκὸν καὶ διφρυγὲς καὶ ἑψήσας πάλιν, ἕως μὴ μολύνῃ, ἔσθ’ ὅτε γὰρ 780 τούτων βληθέντων ἐπανίεται, εἶτα βαστάσας προσδίδου κηρὸν καὶ πάλιν ἕψε, ἕως ἀμόλυντος γένηται, ταχέως δὲ γίνεται, εἶτα ἄρας ἀποδώσεις πρόπολιν καὶ ἐπιθεὶς πάλιν, μικρόν τε ἐάσας καὶ ἄρας ἀποδίδου ἀλόην, μάνναν, δίκταμνον, ἀριστολοχίαν, γεντιανήν. ἐψυγμένων δ’ ἱκανῶς τούτων, καθ’ ἓν ἕκαστον λειότατον ἐμπάσεις καὶ κινήσας ἐὰν ᾖ ἀμόλυντος, βαστάσεις. ἐὰν δὲ μὴ, ἐπὶ κούφου πυρὸς ἑψήσεις. ταῦτα μὲν ὁ Ἥρας ἔγραψε περὶ τῆς ἐμπλάστρου. συνεθήκαμεν δ’ αὐτὴν ἡμεῖς ἐνίοτε καὶ χωρὶς τοῦ κεκαυμένου χαλκοῦ καὶ διφρυγοῦς, ἑλκτικωτέραν μὲν τῶν ἐν τῷ βάθει καὶ μαλακτικωτέραν καὶ διαφορητικωτέραν γινομένην, οὐ μὴν πρός γε τὰ δυσεπούλωτα τῶν ἑλκῶν ἁρμόττουσαν. ἐποιήσαμεν δὲ αὐτὴν καὶ χωρὶς τοῦ δικτάμνου, πάλιν αὐτὴν προσθέντες τοῖς ἄλλοις, χρυσοκόλλης δραχ. στ΄. ὡς γενέσθαι θεραπευτικωτέραν τῶν κακοήθων ἑλκῶν. [῾Η Ἱκέσιος μέλαινα.] Ἡ Ἱκέσιος ὀνομαζομένη. τινὲς δὲ κατὰ γενικὴν πτῶσιν ἡ Ἱκεσίου γεγράφασι, πολύχρηστον φάρμακον. ὁ γοῦν Ἥρας περὶ αὐτῆς γράφει ταῦτα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπανίεται, πάλιν αὐτὴν προσθέντες, ἐψυγμένων δ’ ἱκανῶς τούτων

Boil in a large pan over charcoals the litharge and the oil, until it is non-staining entirely or very nearly so; then, having lifted off the pot, mix in the verdigris and the scale and boil, and let it not stain at all; then, having removed it again, put in the resin and the ammoniac, sifted, stirring until it stops boiling; then, having set it on again and left it a little while, remove it and sprinkle in the copper and the diphryges, and having boiled it again until it does not stain — for sometimes, 780 when these have been put in, it is loosened again — then, having removed it, add the wax and boil again until it becomes non-staining, and this happens quickly; then, having lifted it off, you will add the propolis, and having set it on again and left it a little while, lift it off and add the aloe, manna, dittany, birthwort, gentian. When these have cooled sufficiently, you will sprinkle in each one separately, very finely ground, and having stirred, if it is non-staining, you will take it off; but if not, you will boil it over a light fire. These are the things Heras wrote about the plaster. We ourselves have sometimes compounded it also without the burnt copper and the diphryges, it becoming more attractive of what lies in the depth and more emollient and more discutient, though not, to be sure, suited to the ulcers hard to cicatrize. We have also made it without the dittany, again adding to the other ingredients, in its case, six drachms of chrysocolla, so that it becomes more therapeutic of malignant ulcers. [The black Hikesios.] The one named Hikesios — some have written it in the genitive case, "the one of Hikesios" — is a polychrēston (of many uses) drug. Heras, at any rate, writes the following about it.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπανίεται, πάλιν αὐτὴν προσθέντες, ἀμόλυντον, Ἱκέσιος

Boil in a large pan over coals the litharge and the oil until it is entirely non-staining, or nearly so; then, lifting off the pot, mix in the verdigris and the scale and boil, and let it not stain at all; then, taking it off again, put in the resin and the ammoniac, sifted, stirring until it stops boiling; then, setting it back on and leaving it a little, take it off and sprinkle in the copper and the diphryges, and boil again until it does not stain, for sometimes, 780 when these have been thrown in, it slackens again; then, taking it off, add the wax and boil again until it becomes non-staining, and it becomes so quickly; then, lifting it off, you will add the propolis, and setting it on again and leaving it a little, lift it off and add the aloe, manna, dittany, aristolochia and gentian. When these have cooled sufficiently, you will sprinkle each one in separately, ground very smooth, and having stirred, if it is non-staining, you will take it off; if not, you will boil it over a light fire. This is what Heras wrote about the plaster. We ourselves have sometimes compounded it also without the burnt copper and the diphryges, and it becomes more drawing of what lies in the depth, and more emollient and more discutient, yet not suited to the hard-to-cicatrise sores. We have also made it without the dittany, adding in turn in its place, along with the others, 6 dr. of chrysocolla, so that it becomes more therapeutic of malignant sores. [The black plaster of Hikesios.] The one named the Hikesian; some, however, have written it in the genitive case, 'that of Hikesios', a polychrest drug. Heras at any rate writes the following about it.

The Hikesios 781 (plaster): for wounds and dog-bites, and dispersing indurations; for parotid swellings and condylomata and for malignant ulcers. It is also drawing (epispastic) and agglutinating, and it works also for dropsies. ♃ Litharge 100 dr.; Jewish asphalt 100 dr.; ampelitis earth 100 dr.; manna, propolis, 25 dr. each; galbanum 12 dr.; split alum 12½ dr.; terebinth resin 12½ dr.; iris 6 dr.; raw-pressed (omotribes) oil 6 kotylai; honey 1 kotyle; chalkitis 7½ dr.; omphakitis gall-nut 6 dr.; myrrh 6 dr. Boil the litharge and the asphalt and the ampelitis with the oil. When they are half-boiled, mix in the propolis and the resin and the galbanum, boiled separately in the honey. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: λελειοτριβημένῳ, ἀκάνθης, διειμένη

782 The black plaster made with the two aristolochias, effective for old, callous, hard-to-cicatrize and spreading sores. It is also a haemostatic, a cephalic plaster, it draws up bones, is agglutinating, effective for slackened joints, it cleanses parts afflicted with flux, and fills up when diluted. Pitch, one litra; wax, one litra; bitumen, one litra; terebinth resin, one litra; manna, half a litra or seven ounces; scraped verdigris, half a litra; white lead, half a litra; chalcanthum, four ounces; long aristolochia, four ounces; Illyrian iris, four ounces; pomegranate rind, two ounces; omphakitis gall-nut, two ounces; burnt copper, two ounces; akantha, four ounces; round aristolochia, four ounces; vinegar, one kotyle; castor oil or old olive oil, one kotyle. Having melted the meltable ingredients together, then drip in the white lead, the verdigris and the chalcanthum, finely triturated with the vinegar; then, having set it on the fire until it is unified, sprinkle in the dry ingredients after drawing away the fire; then pour it out and knead. These things, then, are what Heras and the others have written about it.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔχοντος δὲ καὶ λιβανωτοῦ ταῦτα προσείληφεν ἡ μάννα στυπτικὴν ὅλην

The reasoning of the one who composed it is not unclear to those 783 who remember what has been said by me on this very point both in the preceding books of this work. For it is composed of opposite powers, attractive and repellent, which have drying in common. What predominates in it is the element of the repellents, which belongs to the astringents: the chalkanthon, the pomegranate rinds, the oak-gall, the burnt copper and the thorn (akantha). For this reason he also said that it is suitable for joints that are paralysed (paralelymena), that is, slackened. The aristolochia and the iris he mixed with the astringents, these being of rarefying and discutient power; and so that the drug might become a plaster and not be a dry dusting-powder (epipaston), oil and wax were mixed in. Wax only as material, as also the oil—if this is the common oil and not castor oil or old oil; for we have learned that these heat and disperse. Terebinth resin, in addition to producing the plaster-like consistency for the drug, since wax alone cannot do this, also has something emollient and discutient and detergent. And while frankincense also has these properties, the manna has acquired in addition a wholly astringent power and quality.

The reasoning of the man who composed it is not obscure to those 783 who remember all that I have said, at least in this treatise and also in the preceding commentaries. For it is compounded of opposite faculties, drawing and repelling, which have drying in common. In it the repelling component predominates, the one belonging to the astringents: chalcanthum, pomegranate rinds, oak-gall, burnt copper and thorn. For this reason he also said that it is suitable for paralysed joints, that is, relaxed ones. He mixed the birthwort and the iris with the styptic agents, these being of a rarefying and dispersive faculty. And so that it might become a plaster and the drug not be a dry sprinkling-powder, oil and wax were mixed in: the wax merely as material, as also the oil, if this is the common oil and not castor oil or old oil, for we have learned that these heat and disperse. Terebinth resin, besides producing the plaster-like consistency in the drug (since wax alone cannot do this), also has something emollient, dispersive and detergent about it. Frankincense too has these properties, and manna has in addition taken on a wholly styptic faculty and quality.

For that is what they call 784 the shaken-off residue (aposeisma) of frankincense, which partakes also to some small degree of the bark. Sufficiently drying, too, is asphalt (hē asphaltos or ho asphaltos, whichever way you wish to call it), a drug suitable for the composition of a plaster. These, then, were sufficient for the composition of the plaster. But the one who composed this plaster, having set out to mix in drugs possessing a strong power, added to them verdigris and vinegar, which are capable of cutting and eroding, and both of being carried themselves to the depth of the bodies and of guiding the others there. These are mixed with the discutient drugs for the sake of thinning beforehand the thick moistures in the parts being treated. It remains, then, to say something also about the white lead, which, as is quite clear, he mixed in as suitable for plaster-making, not as something that would itself contribute anything. I, however, would rather have mixed in litharge, for it is both pore-blocking (emplastic) and cooling. White lead, on the other hand, though useful for certain other purposes, contributes nothing to the power of the plaster under discussion. The 785 [plaster] of Azanites. Take: dry pitch, 1 ounce; wax, 3 ounces; pig's fat, 3 ounces; bull's fat, 3 ounces; oesypum, 3 ounces; pine resin, 5 ounces. It is effective for all malignant conditions, gangrenes, fistulas, fresh wounds, burns, chillings of the extremities (peripsygmoi), [used] as an acopon. It is also an emollient pessary. It is suited too for ulcers in the womb, phymata, parotid swellings. When the ulcers are hollow, use the drug in greater quantity; but when [it is used] for cicatrization, less. It is a peculiarity of the drug that it produces a filthy crust (epipagos). It is also effective in those suffering from ophthalmia, when applied to the eyelids. Oil is antipathetic to the drug, therefore be on your guard. When you prepare it, put in the pitch, the wax, the bull's fat; and when they have melted, the pig's fat, then the resin, and last the oesypum, after cooling. Melt the whole over fire and not over flame. It is especially effective in those in whom the blow is around the sinews or a muscle, and in malignant cases.

[The plaster of Halieus, by means of Sinopian earth.] The plaster of Halieus, famous and of many uses (polychrēston) no less than the preceding ones, written thus 786 by Heras. The plaster of Halieus, for old and hard-to-cicatrize ulcers. It is also discutient (diaphorētikē), and especially for indurations around the breasts. We use it as the best for most conditions. Of wax 1 mina; of litharge 1 mina; of manna 8 drachms; of terebinth 8 drachms; of galbanum 8 drachms; of Sinopian earth 8 drachms; of old oil 2 cotylae. Boil the oil and the litharge until it becomes non-staining, then the wax, the resin; and when these have melted, having taken it off the fire, mix in the Sinopian earth, the galbanum, then the manna. This also works extremely well for wounds inflicted by sea-creatures. Indeed, I once used it on someone who had been struck by a sea stingray and was suffering most dreadfully, and it worked marvellously; likewise also on those stung by scorpions. These things, then, Heras wrote. Next let us look at those of Criton. [On the things written by Criton.] Heras composed one book on the composition of drugs, entitled Narthex. But five such books of Criton survive, apart from the Cosmetics.

[The plaster of Halieus, made with Sinopic earth.] The plaster of Halieus, celebrated and polychrest no less than the preceding ones, recorded thus 786 by Heras. The plaster of Halieus, for old ulcers and those hard to cicatrise. It is also discutient, especially for indurations about the breasts. We use it as an excellent remedy for very many things. Wax, 1 mina; litharge, 1 mina; manna, 8 drachms; terebinth, 8 dr.; galbanum, 8 dr.; Sinopic earth, 8 dr.; old oil, 2 cotylae. Boil the oil and the litharge until it becomes non-staining, then add the wax and the resin. When these have melted, take the vessel off the fire and mix in the Sinopic earth and the galbanum, then the manna. This plaster also acts supremely well against the wounds of sea creatures. I once used it on a man who had been struck by a sea stingray and was suffering terribly, and it acted marvellously; likewise also on those stung by scorpions. This is what Heras wrote. Next let us look at Crito's prescriptions. [On what was written by Crito.] Heras composed a single book on the composition of drugs, entitled Narthex. Five such books of Crito survive, apart from his Cosmetics.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προχυλώσας, προβληθέντων, μελανίας, φύγεθλα

The (plaster) of Hicesius: for every wound, fresh or old; for contusions, sprains, fractures; for inflammations and every pain — for it makes (the part) painless at once; for abscesses, phymata, scrofulous swellings, carbuncles, parotid swellings, phygethla, condylomata, lichens, corns, black marks (melaniai), inflamed haemorrhoids, fistulas, burns, when it is loosened with myrtle oil or rose oil; 788 for ulcers, splinters, arrow-points, dog bites, human bites, wounds from wild beasts; use it also as a haemostatic (ischaimos) and in place of a malagma for everything — but do not apply oil — and as an anakollema on patients suffering from ophthalmia, on a piece of linen. Let the compresses (splenia) always be rather large. It is effective also in dropsical patients. Take: ampelitis earth, asphalt, litharge, 2 minae each; wax, frankincense, terebinth, propolis, 1/2 mina each; galbanum, split alum, 5 minae each; myrrh, gall-nut, iris, 12 drachmae each; chalcitis, 6 drachmae; honey, 2 cotylae; omphacine oil, 2 choes, but some say 2 cotylae. Boil the ampelitis, the asphalt and the litharge with the oil, having first worked them into a pulp (προχυλώσας), so that they become smooth. Boil over a gentle fire, stirring continuously with a spatula of pine-wood, so that it does not settle to the bottom, for it burns very quickly. Melt the meltable ingredients in another vessel, having first boiled the honey separately so that it thickens, and mix them with the ingredients in the kettle once these have been well thickened; for it dissolves again when these are added (προβληθέντων); then sprinkle in the dry ingredients when you have cooled it somewhat, last of all the frankincense, and, having united the whole, use it.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἥλους μελανίας, προχυλώσας, ἀνακόλλημα, μνᾶς ε΄, πάλιν γὰρ ἀναλύεται τούτων προβληθέντων

Hicesius' [plaster]: for every wound, fresh or old; for contusions, sprains and fractures; for inflammations and every pain, for it at once renders [the part] free of pain; for abscesses, phymata, choirades, carbuncles, parotid swellings, phygethla, condylomata, lichens, corns, melaniai, inflamed haemorrhoids, fistulas and burns, when it is softened with myrtle or rose oil; 788 for ulcers, splinters, barbs, dog bites, human bites and the wounds of wild beasts; it is also haemostatic. Use it also in place of a malagma for everything, but do not apply oil; and as an anacollema for those suffering from ophthalmia, on a linen cloth. Let the compresses always be larger. It is also effective for dropsical patients. ℞ Ampelitis earth, asphalt, litharge, 2 minas of each; wax, frankincense, terebinth, propolis, ½ mina of each; galbanum, split [alum], 5 minas of each; myrrh, oak gall, iris, 12 drachmas of each; chalcitis, 6 drachmas; honey, 2 kotylai; omphacine oil, 2 choes (some say 2 kotylai). Boil the ampelitis, the asphalt and the litharge with the oil, having first reduced them to a juice so that they become smooth. Boil over a gentle fire, stirring continuously with a spatula of pine wood so that it does not settle at the bottom, for it burns very quickly. Melt the meltable ingredients in another vessel, having first boiled the honey separately until it thickens, and mix them with the contents of the pot once these have thickened properly; for when these are thrown in, it dissolves again. Then sprinkle in the dry ingredients after cooling it somewhat, the frankincense last, and when you have unified it, use it.

Greek (Kühn)

ταύτην τὴν γραφὴν ἔλαβον παρὰ 789 Ἡροδότου. ταῦτα μὲν ἔγραψεν ὁ Κρίτων περὶ τῆς Ἱκεσίου. φαίνεται δὲ διαφερόμενος τῷ Ἥρᾳ περὶ τῆς συμμετρίας τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων, ἐξ ὧν ὅλη σύγκειται. καὶ τῆς διαφωνίας ἡ μέν τις ἐστὶ σαφὴς, ἡ δὲ δυσεπίκριτος. ὁ μὲν γὰρ Ἥρας καλῶς ποιῶν τὸν σταθμὸν ἁπάντων τῶν φαρμάκων εἰς δραχμὰς ἀνῆγεν, ὁ δὲ Κρίτων τὴν μὲν ἀμπελῖτιν γῆν καὶ τὴν ἄσφαλτον καὶ τὴν λιθάργυρον οὐ κατὰ τὸν τῶν δραχμῶν σταθμὸν ἔγραψεν, ἀλλὰ μνᾶς β΄. ἑκάστης αὐτῶν ἠξίωσεν ἐμβάλλειν. διαπεφώνηται δὲ τοῖς περὶ τῶν σταθμῶν καὶ μέτρων γράψασιν, ὁπόσος ἐστὶν ὁ τῆς μνᾶς σταθμὸς, ἐνίων μὲν ἑκκαίδεκα λεγόντων οὐγγιῶν εἶναι τὴν μνᾶν, ἐνίων δὲ εἴκοσι, ἐνίων δὲ καὶ διοριζομένων, καὶ τὴν μὲν Ἀλεξανδρικὴν εἴκοσι φασκόντων εἶναι οὐγγιῶν, τὴν δὲ ἄλλην ἑκκαίδεκα, καὶ τοῦτο μὲν ἔτι μικρότερον. ἀλλὰ τῶν εἰς δραχμὰς ἀναγόντων τὴν μνᾶν εἰσὶν οἵ φασιν ἑκατὸν εἷναι δραχμῶν τὴν μνᾶν, ἔνιοι δὲ πλειόνων, ἐπειδὴ καὶ τὴν οὐγγίαν οἱ πλεῖστοι μὲν ἑπτὰ καὶ ἡμίσεος δραχμῶν εἶναί φασιν, ἔνιοι δὲ ζ΄. μόνον, ἕτεροι δὲ η΄.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: καὶ τοῦτο μὲν ἔτι μικρότερον

I received this recipe from 789 Herodotus. These are the things Criton wrote about the plaster of Hicesius. But he appears to differ from Heras concerning the proportion of the simple drugs from which the whole is composed. And of the disagreement, one part is clear, the other hard to adjudicate. For Heras, acting rightly, reduced the weight of all the drugs to drachmas, whereas Criton wrote the ampelitis earth and the asphalt and the litharge not according to the weight in drachmas, but required that two minae of each of them be put in. But there is disagreement among those who have written on weights and measures as to how great the weight of the mina is, some saying that the mina is sixteen ounces, some twenty, and some also making a distinction and asserting that the Alexandrian mina is twenty ounces and the other sixteen; and this is a still smaller matter. But among those who reduce the mina to drachmas there are some who say that the mina is a hundred drachmas, and some say more, since the ounce too most say is seven and a half drachmas, but some only seven, and others eight.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καὶ τοῦτο μὲν ἔτι μικρότερον, γραφὴν

This formula I received from 789 Herodotus. These things, then, Criton wrote about the [plaster] of Hicesius. He appears to differ from Heras on the proportion of the simple drugs of which the whole is compounded. Of this disagreement one part is clear, the other hard to decide. For Heras, rightly, reduced the weight of all the drugs to drachmas, whereas Criton did not write the vine-earth, the asphalt and the litharge by the weight in drachmas, but directed that 2 minas of each of them be put in. Now those who have written on weights and measures disagree about how great the weight of the mina is: some say that the mina is of sixteen ounces, some of twenty, and some even draw a distinction, asserting that the Alexandrian mina is of twenty ounces and the other of sixteen. And this is a still smaller matter. But among those who reduce the mina to drachmas, there are some who say that the mina is of a hundred drachmas, and some of more, since, as regards the ounce too, most say it is of seven and a half drachmas, but some of only 7, and others of 8.

These things being so, then, it is difficult to find out how many drachmas 790 one ought to reckon the mina written down by Criton to be. He appears, however, in other passages too to be one who wants the mina to be 100 drachmas. And if this is so, one must understand that the composition of the plaster of Hicesius has been written by him at double, since Heras, writing about these first three drugs—ampelitis earth, asphalt and litharge—gave the weight of each as one hundred drachmas, whereas Criton raises it to 200. Let us see, then, whether what follows agrees with the double; for some items appear to, others do not. After the first three he mentions wax, frankincense, terebinth and propolis, requiring that half a mina of each be put in, that is, 50 drachmas. For we laid it down that the proportion has been written on the assumption that the mina is 100 drachmas. But if this is so, in the case of the frankincense—evidently written in place of manna—there will be agreement between Criton and Heras. Likewise too in the case of propolis, but certainly not in the case of terebinth. For twenty-five drachmas ought

to reckon it, as others direct, knowing well that, if the drug should be boiled for a longer time—and this will happen when more 793 oil has been put in—it will become more drying, whereas if it should be taken quickly from the fire, because of the small quantity of the oil it will become less drying, but more emollient and more soothing; so that the six kyathoi must be rejected altogether, and six kotylai, each one of nine ounces, must be put in. Greek (Kühn)

ἡ ἴσις, ἥν τινες Ἐπιγόνου καλοῦσι, ποιοῦσα, ἄκρατος μὲν πρὸς λεπίδων ἀποστάσεις καὶ ἀναπλεύσεις ὀστέων παχείας, ἔμμοτος δὲ καὶ σπλήνιον ἐπιτιθεμένη καὶ πρὸς σκόλοπας καὶ θηρίων πάντων πληγάς τε καὶ δήγματα καὶ πρὸς πάσας τὰς ὑπὸ σιδήρου γινομένας ἐν πολέμοις τε καὶ ἄλλως νύξεις τε καὶ τρώσεις θεραπεύει καὶ σύριγγας ἐπιτιθεμένη καὶ κολλύριον ἐντιθεμένη, ἐκτυλοῦσα καὶ παρακολλῶσα ταῦτα, καὶ κεκραμένη δὲ αὕτη πρὸς πᾶν τραῦμα ἔμμοτον καὶ σπλήνιον ἐπιτιθεμένη καὶ πρὸς τὰ κακοήθη καὶ χρόνια καὶ ὑπ’ ἄλλων κατεφθαρμένα, ἐφ’ ὧν προαναξέων τῷ φαρμάκῳ χρῶ τῇ 795 κεκραμένῃ. ποιεῖ δὲ θαυμαστῶς καὶ πρὸς τὰς παρακολλήσεις τῶν κόλπων, ἕως ἂν ὦσι μεγάλοι. ἐξικμάζουσα γὰρ καὶ παρακολλῶσα τούτους εὐθετεῖ καὶ ἔμμοτος καὶ κεκραμένη καὶ ἄκρατος, ἐφ’ ὧν ὑπεκτῆξαι θέλομεν ἀδένας τινὰς ἢ ὑμένας ἐγκειμένους. χρῶ δὲ καὶ πρὸς τὰ κατάγματα σὺν τραύματι καὶ χωρὶς τραύματος. πωροῖ γὰρ τάχιστα καὶ σφίγγει τὰ πεπονθότα. καὶ ἐφ’ ὧν δ’ ἄλλο μάλαγμα οὐ ποιεῖ, αὕτη ἀκριβῶς ποιεῖ, ὄνυχάς τε ἐκ προσκόμματος σεσαλευμένους ἢ πήσσει ἢ ἐκτινάσσει καὶ ὀδόντας ἐκ πληγῆς κεκινημένους πήσσει, καὶ καθόλου μύριά ἐστιν ἐφ’ ὧν ποιεῖ διαφόρως ἀγομένη. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἡ ἀνειμένη αὕτη τῆς ἀκράτου μέρος ἓν, κηρωτῆς μέρη β΄. ἐνίοτε καὶ ἔτι μᾶλλον ἐξανειμένη. τριβῆς δὲ εἰς τοῦτο καὶ γυμνασίας δεῖ. γίνεται γὰρ ἡ κηρωτή ποτε καὶ διὰ πιτυΐνης ῥητίνης, ἡ δὲ σύνθεσις, ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος δραχ. η΄. ἰοῦ ξυστοῦ δραχ. η΄. ἁλὸς ἀμμωνιακοῦ δραχ. η΄. λεπίδος χαλκοῦ δραχ. η΄. χαλκοῦ κεκαυμένου δραχ. η΄. στυπτηρίας στρογγύλης δραχ. στ΄. ἀριστολοχίας δραχ. η΄. λιβάνου δραχ. η΄.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀναπλεύσεις, διαφόρως ἀγομένη, ὑπ’ ἄλλων κατεφθαρμένα

The Isis, which some call Epigonus' drug, is effective: undiluted, against separations of scales and thick exfoliations of bones; applied on lint and as a splenium, both against splinters and the blows and bites of all wild creatures, and it heals all the punctures and wounds inflicted by iron, in wars and otherwise, and fistulas, when applied on top and inserted as a collyrium, removing the callus of these and gluing them together; and this same drug, diluted, for every wound, applied on lint and as a splenium, and for malignant and chronic wounds and those spoiled by others, in which cases scrape beforehand and use the 795 diluted drug. It works wonderfully also for the agglutination of sinuses, so long as they are large; for by drawing out the moisture and gluing these together it is well suited, both on lint and diluted and undiluted, in cases where we wish to melt away certain glands or membranes lying within. Use it also for fractures, with a wound and without a wound; for it forms callus very quickly and binds tight the affected parts. And in cases where another malagma does not work, this works exactly: nails loosened by a stumble it either fixes firm or casts off, and teeth loosened by a blow it fixes firm; and in general there are countless conditions for which it works when handled in different ways. The relaxed form of it is: of the undiluted one part, of cerate two parts; sometimes it is relaxed still further. For this, practice and exercise are required. For the cerate is also sometimes made with pine resin. The composition: of ammoniac incense, 8 drachms; of scraped verdigris, 8 drachms; of ammoniac salt, 8 drachms; of scale of copper, 8 drachms; of burnt copper, 8 drachms; of round alum, 6 drachms; of aristolochia, 8 drachms; of frankincense, 8 drachms.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παχείας (construction unclear: 'thick' exfoliations, or with λεπίδων), ἀναπλεύσεις, ἕως ἂν ὦσι μεγάλοι (sense odd; perhaps corrupt, expected 'even if large'), ἐκτυλοῦσα, διαφόρως ἀγομένη, ἔμμοτον (agreeing with τραῦμα)

The isis, which some call Epigonus' isis, is effective. Undiluted, it is for separations of scales and for thick exfoliations of bones. Applied on lint and as a compress, it treats splinters, the strikes and bites of all wild animals, and all punctures and wounds made by iron, in war or otherwise. It also treats fistulas, applied over them and inserted as a collyrium, removing their callus and agglutinating them. Mixed, it is applied on lint and as a compress for every wound, and for malignant and chronic wounds and those ruined by other practitioners; in these, scrape them first and then use the drug in its mixed form. 795 It works wonderfully also for the agglutination of sinuses, so long as they are large. For by drawing off their moisture and agglutinating them it puts them in good order, whether on lint, mixed or undiluted, in cases where we wish to melt away from beneath certain glands or membranes lying within. Use it also for fractures, with a wound and without a wound, for it forms callus very quickly and binds the affected parts together. And in cases where no other malagma is effective, this one is precisely effective: nails loosened by a stumble it either fixes or throws off, and teeth moved by a blow it fixes; and in general there are countless conditions for which it is effective when brought to different strengths. The relaxed form of it is one part of the undiluted drug to two parts of cerate; sometimes it is relaxed still further. For this, grinding and working are needed. For the cerate is sometimes also made with pine resin. The composition: ammoniac incense, 8 dr.; scraped verdigris, 8 dr.; ammoniac salt, 8 dr.; flakes of copper, 8 dr.; burnt copper, 8 dr.; round alum, 6 dr.; birthwort, 8 dr.; frankincense, 8 dr.

of myrrh 796 12 drachms, of aloe 12 drachms, of galbanum 12 drachms, of wax 8, of Colophonian resin 6 drachms (some, however, [use] the same amount of terebinth), of old oil 2 cotylae, of vinegar 2 cotylae. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

[The plaster of Machairion.] It is at once renowned and of many uses (polychrēston); it is suited also to those conditions of ulcers about which 797 this whole treatise is being written. Criton wrote it thus, word for word. The plaster of Machairion, effective for all fresh wounds, agglutinating them and keeping them free of inflammation, and especially those accompanied by contusion. In cases of those bitten by rabid dogs it is excellent, applied over a wider area and changed very frequently, and taken as a pill about the size of a bean for 30 days. For every spreading sore (nomē), and especially the phagedaenic kind, and for ulcers in the ears and in the genitals; on denuded bones it makes flesh grow, and (it is good) for ulcers in the nostrils. It is also green. It is diluted with rose-oil or omphacine oil. ℞ round aristolochia 1 oz.; galbanum 1 oz.; opopanax 1 oz.; scraped verdigris 1 oz.; Illyrian iris 1 oz.; ammoniac incense 1 oz.; myrrh 1 oz.; terebinth 7½ oz.; wax 7½ oz.; omphacine oil 7½ oz. Triturate half of the oil with the verdigris; boil the rest with the wax and the terebinth.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γο, S", λυσσοδήκτων, ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος, χλωρά

[That of Machairion.] It is at once renowned and polychrest; it is suited also to the conditions of ulcers, about which 797 this whole treatise is being written. Criton wrote it out thus, word for word: That of Machairion, effective for all fresh wounds, agglutinating them and keeping them free of inflammation, and especially those accompanied by contusion. For those bitten by rabid animals it is excellent, applied over a larger area and changed very frequently, and also taken as a pill, about the size of a bean, for 30 days. It is effective for every spreading ulceration, and especially the phagedaenic, and for ulcers in the ears and in the genitals; on denuded bones it makes flesh grow; and it is effective for ulcers in the nostrils. It is also green. It is dissolved with rose oil or omphacine oil. ℞ Round birthwort, 1 oz.; galbanum, 1 oz.; opopanax, 1 ounce; scraped verdigris, 1 oz.; Illyrian iris, 1 oz.; ammoniac incense, 1 ounce; myrrh, 1 ounce; terebinth, 7½ ounces; wax, 7½ oz.; omphacine oil, 7½ oz. Triturate half of the oil with the verdigris until smooth, and boil the rest with the wax and the terebinth.

[Δυσραχῖτις πρὸς λέξιν ὡς Δαμοκράτης.] Καὶ αὕτη ﻿ 798 τῶν ἐνδόξων τε καὶ πολυχρήστων ἐστὶ φαρμάκων. γράφει δὲ περὶ αὐτῆς ὁ Κρίτων οὕτως. δυσραχῖτις, παραδόξως ποιοῦσα ἐπὶ τῶν κόλπων τῶν περιτετυλωμένων καὶ συρίγγων ἐπιτιθεμένη. ἐὰν δὲ στόμιον ᾖ, δυνάμενον παραδέξασθαι ὀθονίδιον κεχρισμένον τῷ φαρμάκῳ ἐντίθει καὶ τάχιον ἐνεργήσει. χρῶ δὲ τῇ δυνάμει ἐπὶ τῶν καθύγρων καὶ ῥευματικῶν καὶ χρονίων διαθέσεων, διὰ τρίτης τὰ πολλὰ ἀλλάττων αὐτήν. θαυμαστῶς δὲ ἐνεργεῖ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ποδαγρικῶν ἐν ἀρχῇ τοῦ παροξυσμοῦ ἐπιπλασσομένη καὶ ἐκ πλάτους περιτιθεμένη, οἱ καὶ ἀπολυέσθωσαν ἐπὶ τοὺς συνήθεις περιπάτους καὶ τὰς τοῦ βίου χρείας δίχα βαλανείου, ἕως τῆς διὰ τρίτης καὶ ἐπί τινας ἡμέρας ἐπιμενέτωσαν. οὐ γὰρ δίδωσι τῇ ὕλῃ πάροδον εἰς ἐπίτασιν τοῦ πάθους. ἁρμόζει δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ παντὸς ἕλκους πρὸς ἓξ μέρη κηρωτῇ μιγνυμένη. παλαιουμένη δ’ ἡ δύναμις γίγνεται κρείσσων. ♃ στυπτηρίας στρογγύλης δραχ. η΄. λεπίδος δραχ. ιβ΄. χαλκοῦ κεκαυμένου δραχ. δ΄. ἀριστολοχίας δραχ. θ΄. χαλβάνης δραχ. ι΄. ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος δραχ. ιβ΄. οἱ δὲ δραχ. ι΄. λιβάνου δραχ. θ΄. σμύρνης δραχ. θ΄. στύρακος δραχ. α΄. μίσυος δραχ. γ΄.

[The Dysrachitis, word for word as Damocrates gives it.] This too 798 is one of the famous and widely useful drugs. Criton writes about it as follows. The dysrachitis, acting remarkably when applied upon callused sinuses and fistulas. If there is an opening capable of admitting a small piece of linen smeared with the drug, insert it, and it will act more quickly. Use the preparation on very moist, rheumatic and chronic conditions, changing it for the most part every third day. It also acts wonderfully on gouty patients when it is spread on as a plaster at the beginning of the paroxysm and applied around over a broad area; and let them be released to their customary walks and the business of daily life without the bath, until the third day, and let them continue for some days. For it does not give the matter passage toward an intensification of the affection. It is also suitable for every ulcer when mixed with cerate in the proportion of six parts. As the preparation ages it becomes stronger. ♃ round alum 8 drachmas; scale 12 drachmas; burnt copper 4 drachmas; aristolochia 9 drachmas; galbanum 10 drachmas; ammoniac incense 12 drachmas, others 10 drachmas; frankincense 9 drachmas; myrrh 9 drachmas; storax 1 drachma; misy 3 drachmas.

[The Dysrhachitis, word for word as Damocrates (gives it).] This too 798 is among the celebrated and much-used drugs. Crito writes about it thus: Dysrhachitis, acting remarkably on callused sinuses and on fistulas when applied. If there is an opening able to receive a small piece of linen smeared with the drug, insert it, and it will act more quickly. Use the preparation on very moist, fluxional and chronic conditions, changing it for the most part every third day. It also acts wonderfully on gouty patients when plastered on at the beginning of the paroxysm and applied all round over a wide area. Let these patients also be released to their customary walks and the business of life, without the bath, until the third-day change, and let them persist with it for some days. For it does not give the matter passage towards an intensification of the affection. It is suitable also for every ulcer when mixed with six parts of cerate. As it ages the preparation becomes stronger. ℞ round alum, 8 dr.; scale, 12 dr.; burnt copper, 4 dr.; birthwort, 9 dr.; galbanum, 10 dr.; ammoniac incense, 12 dr. (others, 10 dr.); frankincense, 9 dr.; myrrh, 9 dr.; storax, 1 dr.; misy, 3 dr.

of chalcitis 799 3 drachms; of the heart of squill 8 drachms; of liquid pitch 4 drachms; of Illyrian iris 9 drachms, others 4; of terebinth resin 10 drachms, others 7 drachms; of pine resin 10 drachms, others 6; of wax 75 drachms; of oil 1½ pounds. Some add also deer marrow, 8 drachms, others 20. Grind beforehand the scale, the copper, the misy, the chalcitis and the squill with very sharp vinegar in the sun, so that they are made smooth. When they have become very finely ground, add the aristolochia and the iris. Having first pounded these and sifted them through a fine-meshed sieve, and having again poured vinegar over them, grind them fine, and do this for as long as any roughness shows itself, not sparing in the consumption of vinegar. When they have become smooth, put in the alum, and do the same until this too has been ground smooth. Then the myrrh, after which the frankincense, then the ammoniac, and after these the storax, then the pitch. Let these be heated in the hot sun, so that the storax may be worked up.

When everything has become smooth and has acquired a thickness like sordes (ῥυπῶδες) and like gloios (γλοιῶδες), melt the wax, the resins and the oil, and, having poured a little of the 800 boiling liquid over the galbanum and united it, grind it in a mortar; and after cooling for a little while, add the melted ingredients, then, having put in the galbanum and dissolved it, strain into an earthenware vessel and, scraping it out, mix it with the things in the mortar. [The plaster made with willows.] The plaster made with willows, which is itself also renowned and a polychreston (of many uses), Crito writes, in the same way as the ones written above, thus: The plaster made with willows, suited for fresh (bleeding) wounds and loosened joints, and for fistulas when inserted into them, and for flabby flesh, especially that on the extremities. It works also for erysipelas and for very many other conditions, except burns. Take misy, chalcitis, verdigris, white lead, oak-galls, split alum and round alum, melanteria, 6 ounces of each; wax, pine resin, pitch, asphalt, willow leaves, 2 ounces of each; oil, 4 kotylai 4 ounces; chalcanthum, 6 ounces; pomegranate rinds, 6 ounces; vinegar, 6 xestai. Boil the willows with vinegar until 2 xestai are left.

802 The plaster of Halieus. Tawny. It works for bloody wounds and for severings of nerves, and for ulcers that are old, hard to cicatrize, and rheumatic. It is also a good emollient, especially for the indurations that arise around the breasts. We use it also on those bitten by dogs and those bitten by human beings. It works supremely also for the strokes of sea stingrays and weevers and of the other dangerous fishes, and in general its power is marvellous. Take litharge, wax, 100 drachmas each; terebinth, manna of frankincense, galbanum, Sinopic red ochre, 8 drachmas each; oil, 1 xestes. Prepare in the usual manner. Galen's version of the plaster of Halieus. Take litharge, wax, 100 drachmas each; terebinth (the text's τερμινμινθίνης is a corruption of τερμινθίνης), myrrh, Sinopic ochre, scraped verdigris, scale of copper, 8 drachmas each; aristolochia, 8 drachmas; frankincense, 25 drachmas; diphryges, 6 drachmas; galbanum, 12 drachmas; ammoniac incense, 12 drachmas; oil, 2 kotylai. Prepare in the usual manner. The plaster of the hunters. A well-tried drug.

It works also against the wounds and bites of four-footed animals and the remaining [injuries] from venomous creatures. 803 Take litharge, 1 mina; old oil, scale of red copper, wax, roasted resin, 1 litra each; ammoniac incense, 4 ounces; galbanum, Sinopic red earth, 2 ounces each. Boil the litharge, the scale and the oil until they reach consistency, then add the wax and the resin. And when it has melted, having removed it from the fire, add the ammoniac, having flattened it out. And when it has cooled, add the galbanum and ammoniac, sprinkling on the Sinopic earth, and having poured it out into a mortar and kneaded it until soft, use it. The [plaster] of the Chalcidean. Agglutinative, non-inflammatory. It works also against divisions accompanied by contusion, and it glues together sinuses and dries them up and prevents abscessions; it brings up corrupted bones; and it works especially also on the extremities, and in general its power is wonderful. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στ΄. καὶ εἴκοσιν, Ἰλλυρικῆς

The written version of this man differs 805 from that of Heras in respect of the weights in the following points: whereas this man orders that 8 drachms of dittany be put in, Heras ordered ten. And moreover, of aristolochia and of ammoniac incense this man puts 16 drachms of each, but Heras twenty-six of ammoniac incense and 10 of aristolochia. As for the oil, Heras wrote it without distinction, ordering simply 2 kotylai, whereas Asclepiades orders that much in winter, but one and a half in summer. [The plaster] of Diophantus the surgeon, which Phylacus used. Take litharge 150 drachms; white lead, pitch, scale of copper, wax, 18 drachms each; verdigris, dry pine resin, manna of frankincense, propolis, 12 drachms each; galbanum, aristolochia, cyperus, 6 drachms each; dittany, trefoil, ammoniac incense, 3 drachms each; Illyrian iris, artemisia, polion, horehound, 2 drachms each; old oil 2 kotylai. Prepare in the usual manner.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: στ΄. καὶ εἴκοσιν (read as 'twenty-six'; punctuation and numeral likely corrupt), Ἰλλυρικῆς (presumably Illyrian iris; noun understood), πολίου, πρασίου

The recipe of this author 805 disagreed with that of Heras as regards the weights in the following points. Where he directs one to put in 8 dr. of dittany, Heras directed ten. And indeed also of birthwort and of ammoniac incense this author prescribes 16 dr. of each, but Heras twenty-six of ammoniac incense and 10 of birthwort. As for the oil, Heras wrote of it without distinction, prescribing only 2 kotylai, whereas Asclepiades prescribes that amount in winter, but one and a half in summer. [The plaster] of Diophantus the surgeon, which Phylax used. ♃ Litharge 150 dr.; white lead, pitch, flake of copper, wax, 18 dr. each; verdigris, dry pine resin, manna of frankincense, propolis, 12 dr. each; galbanum, birthwort, cyperus, 6 dr. each; dittany, trefoil, ammoniac incense, 3 dr. each; Illyrian [iris], artemisia, polion, prasion, 2 dr. each; old oil 2 kotylai. Prepare in the usual way.

[On the plasters of the same kind written by Andromachus.] I set out, in this book, to go through plasters that are polychrēsta (of many uses) and at the same time effective against ulcers hard to cicatrize. 806 Among these, then, are also the ten finest of those written by Andromachus. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μολύνῃ, ὄρχεως βοτάνης

808 of dry resin 25 drachms, of ammoniac incense 25 drachms, of terebinth resin 25 drachms, of nitre 25 drachms, of aristolochia 25 drachms, of bryony 12 drachms, of the herb orchis 12 drachms, of dry iris 4 drachms, of Indian aloe 12 drachms, of split alum 8 drachms, of bull's gall 8 drachms, of vinegar one cotyle, of oil 1 cotyle; and when it is smeared on, with rose-oil. A green plaster for herpetes, according to Andromachus, and for Chironian ulcers and those hard to cicatrize. Take of burnt barley 10 drachms, of verdigris 8 drachms, of wax 80 drachms, of henna oil 1 cotyle. This drug contains a great deal of verdigris in relation to the proportion of the wax and the henna oil. For the burnt barley, sprinkled on by itself, benefits the Chironian ulcers, neither intensifying the power of the wax and the henna oil nor weakening that of the verdigris. And if it is removed, the drug becomes similar to many of those written down in the book before this one, in the section on the cure of such ulcers. It will not, however, take a generous amount of henna oil, especially if the wax is oily, but only as much as is necessary for softening the wax that has been mixed with the verdigris.

αἱ προγεγραμμέναι ἔμπλαστροι πᾶσαι διὰ τοῦτο πολύχρηστοι 809 γεγόνασιν, ὅτι φαρμάκων προσειλήφασι μαλακτικῶν, ἑλκτικῶν, διαφορητικῶν πεπτικῶν ἔτι τε τῶν ξηραινόντων ἀδήκτως· αἱ πλεῖσται δὲ αὐτῶν καὶ τῶν ἰσχυρῶς στυφόντων, ὧν δύναμίς ἐστιν ἀποκρουστικὴ τῶν ἐπιῤῥεόντων ὑγρῶν τῷ πεπονθότι μορίῳ. τὰς δ’ ὕλας τῶν εἰρημένων φαρμάκων. δυνάμεως ὀνομαστὶ γεγραμμένας ἔχετε κατὰ τὴν περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων πραγματείαν ἐν ἕκτῳ καὶ ἑβδόμῳ καὶ ὀγδόῳ καὶ θ΄. καὶ ι΄. καὶ ια΄. πολλῶν δὲ ὀνομαστὶ κατὰ τὴν ἐνεστῶσαν πραγματείαν ἐμνημόνευσα καὶ πάλιν μνημονεύσω καὶ μέντοι καὶ τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον ἐνταῦθα προσέγραψα, τούτων αὐτῶν ὧν νῦν εἶπον ἀναμνῆσαι βουλόμενος, ἐπεί τοι καὶ αὐτὸ τοῖς προγεγραμμένοις κατὰ τὸ πρὸ τούτου βιβλίον ὁμογενὲς ὑπάρχει καὶ ἦν οἰκειότερον αὐτὸ σὺν ἐκείνοις γεγράφθαι. [Ἡ Ἱκεσία μέλαινα Ἀνδρομάχου.] Τὴν ἔμπλαστρον ταύτην ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος οὐ κατὰ τὸ χωρίον, καθ’ ὃ τὰς χλωρὰς ἔγραψεν, ἀλλ’ ἀνωτέρω τῆς ἀρχῆς ἐγγὺς ἔγραψε καὶ κατὰ συμμετρίαν πολὺ διαλλάττουσαν, ὡς ἔμπροσθεν ἐδήλωσα, τόν τε Κρίτωνα καὶ τὸν Ἥραν γεγραφέναι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῶν εἰρημένων φαρμάκων. δυνάμεως, κατὰ συμμετρίαν πολὺ διαλλάττουσαν ... γεγραφέναι

All the plasters written above have become of many uses (polychrēsta) 809 for this reason, that they have taken on drugs that are emollient, attractive, discutient, concoctive, and further those that dry without biting; and most of them also drugs that are strongly astringent, whose power is repellent of the humours flowing in upon the affected part. The materials of the aforesaid power of drugs you have written by name in the treatise on simple drugs, in the sixth, seventh, eighth, ninth, tenth and eleventh books. And I have mentioned many of them by name in the present treatise and shall mention them again; and indeed I have added this drug here too, wishing to remind you of these very things which I have just said, since in fact it too is of the same kind as those written before in the book preceding this one, and it would have been more appropriate for it to have been written together with those. [The black Hicesian plaster of Andromachus.] This plaster Andromachus wrote not in the place where he wrote the green ones, but higher up, near the beginning, and in a proportion differing greatly from that in which, as I showed earlier, both Crito and Heras wrote it.

The materials of the drugs mentioned, written down by name according to their power, you have in the treatise on simple drugs, in the sixth, seventh, eighth, ninth, tenth and eleventh books. He wrote it also in a proportion that differs greatly, as I showed earlier, from the one in which both Crito and Heras wrote it.

The 810 recipe runs thus. ♃ Of bitumen, 300 drachmai; of ampelitis earth, 200 drachmai; of litharge, 60 drachmai; of galbanum, 36 drachmai; of terebinth, 36 drachmai; of split alum, 36 drachmai; of propolis, 72 drachmai; of manna, 50 drachmai; of myrrh, 12 drachmai; of iris, 12 drachmai; of chalkitis, 6 drachmai; of oak-gall, 10 drachmai; of oil, one chous and a half; of honey, half a kotyle. They began the recipe with a triple quantity of bitumen, as compared with the recipe written by Heras; for Heras wrote 100 drachmai, whereas this man wrote 300, and in some copies, which are not trustworthy, I found 200. The ampelitis earth, however, is clearly written as double that in Heras. having put in [thirty-six] drachmai, he does not preserve the proportion relative to Heras and Criton, just as also in the case of the split alum; for in this 811 he departs slightly from the exact proportion, which comes out to thirty-seven and a half. [Ὡς ὁ Ταραντῖνος Ἡρακλείδης ἔγραψε τὴν 812 Ἱκεσίου μέλαιναν.] Πρὸς πᾶν τραῦμα καὶ μάλιστα τὰ ἐν τοῖς ἄκροις ποιοῦσα ἄπονα καὶ κολλῶσα καὶ πρὸς κυνόδηκτα καὶ δοθιῆνας καὶ τὰς σκληρίας διαχέουσα καὶ μάλιστα τὰς περὶ τοὺς μαστοὺς καὶ τὰ ἐν δακτυλίῳ κονδυλώματα. ♃ λιθαργύρου δραχ. ρ΄. ἀσφάλτου ἴσον, γῆς ἀμπελίτιδος ἴσον, μάννης δραχ. κε΄. προπόλεως ἴσον, χαλβάνης δραχ. ιβ΄. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σπάθῃ δᾳδίνῃ, στυπτηρίας σχιστῆς, ῥητίνης τερμινθίνης ἴσον

[How Heracleides of Tarentum wrote the 812 black plaster of Hicesius.] For every wound, and especially those on the extremities, rendering them painless and agglutinating them; also for dog-bites and boils, and dispersing indurations, especially those around the breasts, and the condylomata in the anus. Take: litharge, 100 drachmas; asphalt, an equal amount; ampelitis earth, an equal amount; manna, 25 drachmas; propolis, an equal amount; galbanum, 12½ drachmas; split alum, terebinth resin, an equal amount; iris, 6 drachmas; chalcitis, 7½ drachmas; oak-gall, 8 drachmas; myrrh, an equal amount; honey, 1 cotyle; oil of unripe olives (omotribes), 6 cotylai. Boil the litharge and the asphalt and the earth with the oil, stirring with a spatula of pine-wood. When it has set, add the propolis and the resin and the galbanum; let these have been previously boiled in the honey; and once these have been mixed in, immediately lifting the pot from the fire, sprinkle in the dry ingredients. Since Heracleides wrote these things in his books addressed to Antiochis, I wonder how some of those after him did not preserve his text. Heras, then, appears to have followed Heracleides in every respect, except in the myrrh and the oak-gall.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μέλαιναν, δᾳδίνῃ, συστῇ, στυπτηρίας σχιστῆς, ῥητίνης τερμινθίνης ἴσον, ♃

[How Heracleides of Tarentum wrote the 812 black (plaster) of Hicesius.] For every wound, and especially those in the extremities, rendering them painless and agglutinating them; and for dog bites and boils (dothiēnes); and dispersing indurations, especially those about the breasts; and the condylomata in the anus. Take: litharge 100 dr., asphalt the same, ampelitis earth the same, manna 25 dr., propolis the same, galbanum 12½ dr., split alum, terebinth resin the same, iris 6 dr., chalcitis 7½ dr., oak-gall 8 dr., myrrh the same, honey 1 kotyle, omotribes oil (oil of unripe olives) 6 kotylai. Boil the litharge, the asphalt and the earth with the oil, stirring with a spatula of resinous pine-wood. When it has thickened, add the propolis, the resin and the galbanum; these should have been boiled beforehand in the honey. When these have been mixed in, at once take the pot off the fire and sprinkle in the dry ingredients. Since Heracleides wrote this in his books To Antiochis, I wonder how some of those after him did not preserve his text. Heras, then, appears to have followed Heracleides in everything, except in the myrrh and the oak-gall.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προσγεγραφέναι μοι δοκοῦσί τινες κοτύλας β΄, κατατετμημένοις κέρασιν

For whereas Heraclides puts in eight 813 drachms of each of these, Heras took six of each of these. And in Asclepiades, in the second book of the External Remedies, everything has been written in fourfold proportion relative to Heras, together with the fact that twelve kotylai of omphacine oil have also been written, Criton having written two choes. And yet his composition had everything at the double amount, which is why some seem to me to have written in addition two kotylai; but this very thing in turn is at odds to a greater degree both with the truth and with the writing of the Tarentine and of Heras. For Heras wrote 180 drachms, reducing the oil to weight and not to measure, on the basis that the kotyle weighs sixty drachms; for the Attic one at any rate does weigh that, being of nine Italian ounces. For the nine Italian ounces, those marked on the graduated horns, weigh seven and a half weight-ounces, which come to sixty drachms, the single ounce containing eight drachms; so that in this respect too Heras agrees with Heraclides in putting in three kotylai, Asclepiades having written twelve, since everything in his composition has been written at four times the amount.
