For whereas Heraclides puts in eight drachmas of each 813 of these, Heras took six of each of them. And in Asclepiades, in the second book of the External [remedies], everything is written in fourfold proportion relative to Heras, with the further point that twelve kotylai of omphacine oil are written, Crito having written two choes. And yet his composition had everything doubled, which is why some seem to me to have added two kotylai in writing. But this in turn conflicts still more with the truth and with the text of both the Tarentine and Heras. For Heras wrote 180 drachmas, reducing the oil to weight, not to measure, on the basis that the kotyle weighs 60 drachmas. And indeed the Attic kotyle does weigh this, being a kotyle of nine Italian ounces. For the nine Italian ounces, those on the graduated horns, weigh seven and a half ounces by weight, which come to 60 drachmas, since one ounce contains eight drachmas. So in this respect too Heras agrees with Heraclides, putting in three kotylai, while Asclepiades has written twelve, since everything in his composition is written at four times the amount.

But Heras, since 814 the bulk of the drug became large according to the Tarentine's prescription, seems to me for this reason to have written a quarter of all [the quantities]. For one must, as I said, make a slight alteration in the myrrh and the oak-gall. And although others too have written this plaster of Hicesius, those who prepared it more accurately, in accordance both with the Tarentine and with the [prescription] judged by us through boiling to be the true one, have turned out in agreement; but those who [did so] more carelessly, whether in one only or two of the ingredients mixed in or even more, are seen to disagree with Heracleides. Therefore it seemed to me superfluous to mention them. For the drug is to be prepared as Heracleides directed or as Heras [did], for the difference between them is very slight. Heracleides says that the following is the plain [plaster] of Hicesius: Take asphalt 50 drachms, litharge 50 drachms, oil 10 kotylai; boil, and when it sets, add wax 5 drachms, galbanum 5 drachms, myrrh 5 drachms.

[Κυζικηνὴ τοῦ Ἥρα.] Τῶν ἐνδόξων ἐμπλάστρων ἐστὶ καὶ αὕτη, σχεδὸν ὑπὸ πάντων γεγραμμένη, τὰ πλεῖστα μὲν ἐν τῇ συμμετρίᾳ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ὁμολογούμενα γραψάντων αὐτῶν, ἐνίοις δὲ καὶ διαφωνησάντων, 815 ὡς ἔσται φανερὸν ὑπογραψάντων ἡμῶν ὡς ἕκαστος αὐτῶν ἀξιοῖ συντίθεσθαι. Ἥρας μὲν οὖν οὕτως γράφει περὶ τῆς προκειμένης ἐμπλάστρου κατὰ λέξιν. Κυζικηνὴ, ποιοῦσα πρὸς τὰ πρόσφατα τραύματα καὶ παλαιὰ τὰ ὑπόνομα. διαχεῖ μάλιστα τὰς ἐν μαστοῖς σκληρίας, ἐπισπᾶται, ἀνακαθαίρει, πληροῖ, κολλᾷ, ἐπουλοῖ. ποιεῖ καὶ πρὸς καρκινώδεις σκληρίας, λιποῦργα καὶ ἐφ’ ὧν οὐκ ἔστι σμιλίῳ χρήσασθαι, ὡς ἐπὶ τραχήλου, κολλυρίῳ χρῶ. καὶ τὰ ἐν ἀπειλῇ πραΰνει ἀποστήματα, φύματα διαχεῖ καὶ τὰ γαγγλία, τάς τε συναγωγὰς ἀποκορυφοῖ καὶ ῥήσσει. ἀνάγει καὶ βέλη καὶ ὀστᾶ καὶ σκινδαλμούς. ἐκτινάσσει δ’ ἀλύπως καὶ ἐσχάρας. ἐπὶ τῶν ὑπονόμων μεγάλοις δεῖ χρῆσθαι τοῖς σπληνίοις. διαχεῖ καὶ χοιράδας καὶ αἱμοῤῥοΐδας ἐπέχει καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν περὶ κύστιν ἄκρως ποιεῖ. ἔχει δὲ λιβανωτοῦ δραχ. δ΄. σμύρνης δραχ. δ΄. κρόκου δραχ. δ΄. ἴρεως Ἰλλυρικῆς δραχ. δ΄. βδελλίου δραχ. δ΄. λεπίδος δραχμὰς ιβ΄. χαλκάνθου δραχ. δ΄. στυπτηρίας σχιστῆς δραχ. δ΄. στρογγύλης δραχ. δ΄. ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος δραχ. δ΄. μίσυος δραχ. δ΄. σιδίων ῥοᾶς γλυκείας δραχ. δ΄.

[The Cyzicene of Heras.] This too is one of the celebrated plasters, written down by nearly everyone, these authors having for the most part written things that agree as regards the proportion of the simple drugs, but having also disagreed in some, 815 as will be evident when we have set down below how each of them thinks it ought to be compounded. Heras, then, writes as follows, word for word, about the plaster before us. Cyzicene, effective for fresh wounds and for old ones that are undermining (ὑπόνομα). It especially disperses the hardnesses in the breasts, draws, cleanses, fills up, agglutinates, cicatrizes. It is effective also for cancerous hardnesses, lipourga (λιποῦργα), and in cases where it is not possible to use the scalpel, as on the neck, use it in the form of a collyrium. It also soothes abscesses that are threatening, disperses phymata and ganglia, and brings collections to a head and breaks them. It also brings up missiles and bones and splinters. And it painlessly shakes off eschars as well. In the case of the undermining ulcers one must use the large compresses (splenia). It also disperses scrofulous swellings (choirades) and checks haemorrhoids and is supremely effective in affections about the bladder. It contains: frankincense 4 drachms, myrrh 4 dr., saffron 4 dr., Illyrian iris 4 dr., bdellium 4 dr., scale (of copper) 12 drachms, chalcanthum 4 dr., split alum 4 dr., round (alum) 4 dr., ammoniac incense 4 dr., misy 4 dr., rind of sweet pomegranate 4 dr.

But he differs in adding aloe, Heras not having added 817 it, and indeed also the root of panax. For Heras wrote opopanax only; Andromachus, however, not this only but also panax. And they customarily call thus the root of the herb which they name panax. Andromachus has in addition also added the juice of poppy for the sake of freedom from pain, and acacia and flower of roses as astringents. The boiling Heras wrote, as is his custom, but Andromachus omits it, as it is again this man's custom too, since he rarely writes the boiling. Nevertheless, from his adding at the end "two kotylai of common vinegar; wine for the dry ingredients", it is clear that he wishes the dry ingredients to be ground fine with the wine or the vinegar, and then the meltable ingredients, once melted, to be poured over these. Heras, however, made no mention at all of vinegar or wine, and at the end of the boiling he added the following: "Finally, throw the dry ingredients and the opopanax into the cauldron, and having united them, pour out and knead, smearing your hands with iris oil."

But I myself prepare it according to Andromachus' method, triturating the opopanax in the mortar together with the dry ingredients: if I should wish to make the drug stronger 818 and more discutient, mixing in the vinegar during the trituration; but if more astringent and more repellent, the wine. I myself prepare it in Andromachus' manner, grinding the opopanax in the mortar together with the dry ingredients. If I wish to make the drug stronger 818 and more discutient, I mix in the vinegar during the grinding; if I wish to make it more astringent and more repellent, I mix in the wine. For the austere wine that is not old is more astringent, while the old wine that is not austere is more discutient. Having put everything into 819 a bronze vessel, boil over soft ash, stirring until, when a drop of it is let fall into cold water, it has the thickness of honey. [Another, from the writings of Philoxenus.] Take frankincense, myrrh, saffron, Illyrian (iris), bdellium, scale of copper, chalkanthon, chalkitis, round and split alum, foreign misy, ammoniac incense, propolis, oak birdlime (ixos), opopanax, closed pomegranate rinds (σιδίων μεμυκότων), four drachms of each; aristolochia, eight drachms; wax, bull's suet, one hundred drachms of each, which he writes as a mina; terebinth resin, fifteen drachms, which he writes as three quarters. and having put the litharge with the oil 823 into a new, wide-mouthed earthenware pan, boil over a light and gentle fire, stirring with a spatula, until it acquires the thickness of Attic honey. 824 [Περὶ τροχίσκων Ἀσκληπιάδου.] Ὥσπερ δ’ ἐμπλάστρους ἀπὸ τῆς χρόας, ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἀπὸ τῆς κατὰ δύναμιν ἐπαγγελίας, ὠνόμασαν ἔνιοι τῶν ἰατρῶν μηλίνας καὶ λευκὰς καὶ φαιὰς καὶ κιῤῥὰς, οὕτως ἀπὸ τοῦ κατὰ τὴν ἀνάπλασιν σχήματος ὀνομάζουσι τροχίσκους οὐ πρὸς ἓν πάθος, ἀλλὰ πρὸς πάνυ πολλὰ διαφέροντας. ἀκολουθήσαντες οὖν αὐτοῖς καὶ ἡμεῖς ἐφεξῆς γράψομεν πάντας· τούς γε δοκίμους, εἴτε κυκλίσκους, εἴτε τροχίσκους, ἑκατέρως γὰρ αὐτοὺς ὀνομάζουσι. καλῶς δὲ ποιοῦντες ἐφ’ ἑκάστου τὴν ἐπαγγελίαν προέγραψαν, καὶ μάλιστα ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης, οὗ καὶ πρώτου τὰ κατὰ τὸ τρίτον πρὸς τὰ ἐκτὸς γεγραμμένα φάρμακα παραθήσομαι. Τροχίσκοι Ἀσκληπιάδου. τροχίσκοι πολύχρηστοι Ἀριστάρχου Ταρσέως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

824 [On the troches of Asclepiades.] Just as some of the physicians named plasters from their colour, and not from the profession of their power, as quince-yellow, white, grey and tawny, so too they name troches from the shape given them in moulding, though they differ not with respect to one affection but with respect to very many. Following them, therefore, we too shall write out all of them in order—those at least that are approved—whether one calls them cyclisci (small disks) or trochisci (troches), for they name them in both ways. They did well in prefixing to each its profession, and especially Asclepiades, whose drugs written in the third book of those for external conditions I shall set out first. Troches of Asclepiades. Troches of many uses (polychrēsta) of Aristarchus of Tarsus.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διαφέροντας, τὰ κατὰ τὸ τρίτον πρὸς τὰ ἐκτὸς γεγραμμένα φάρμακα

824 [On the troches of Asclepiades.] Just as some of the physicians named plasters from their colour, and not from what they promise to do by their power, calling them quince-yellow, white, grey and tawny, so they name troches (trochiskoi) from the shape given them in the moulding, although these differ from one another and are not for one affection but for a great many. Following them, then, we too shall write all of them in order, at least the approved ones, whether they are called little discs (kykliskoi) or troches (trochiskoi), for they name them in both ways. And they did well to write the promise above each one, especially Asclepiades, whose drugs written in the third book of his work on external [affections] I shall set out first. Troches of Asclepiades. Polychrest troches of Aristarchus of Tarsus.

It works for recent and old ulcers; it works also for teredones [worm-eaten bone], honeycomb sores (keria); it works for fistulas, ears discharging pus, corrupted bones; it works for spreading sores (nomai), reduces every excrescence, removes pterygia; it works for the conditions about the womb, for ozaenae, phagedaenae, condylomata; it works for chronic and hard-to-heal conditions; it works 825 for incipient phthisics, splenetics, coeliacs, dysenterics, being given in the weight of three obols with the appropriate drink. It also benefits synanchic patients and spreading sores of the gums, and in general it is well suited to conditions both external and internal. Take: myrrh 6 drachms, saffron 3 drachms, crocomagma 3 drachms, pomegranate blossoms (kytinoi) 6 drachms, chalcanthum 6 drachms, split alum 6 drachms, aloe 6 drachms; take up in Falernian wine and make troches and dry in the shade. At the time of use, dissolve in a liquid suited to the underlying conditions. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

The one entitled "through 827 the plants" (dia tōn phytōn). ℞ pomegranate rinds 8 dr., myrtle leaves 8 dr., dry walnut shells 8 dr., fruit of thorn (akantha) 8 dr., oak-galls 4 dr., mined salt 8 dr. And when it is in good condition, add myrrh 4 dr., frankincense 2 dr., and form into troches; the use is with vinegar. In Terentius it is entitled "astringent" (styptikē). It works against every spreading ulcer (nomē) and the conditions around the anus (daktylios). ℞ sandarach, misy, chalkanthon, split alum, two drachmas each; myrrh, flakes of copper, aloe, chalkitis, 3 dr. each; pomegranate flowers (kytinoi), rose blossom, oak-galls, 4 dr. each; juice of acacia 8 dr.; pomegranate rinds 8 dr. Grind each separately with water, then, having mixed them together, form into troches. The use is with vinegar. Another, the one with origanum. ℞ origanum 2 dr., olive leaves 2 dr., acacia 2 dr.

From the writings of Largus, a successful drug. ♃ Of litharge or white lead 38 drachms, of split alum 12 drachms, of acacia 8 drachms, of saffron, frankincense, diphryges, opium, 2 drachms each. 841 ♃ Sandarach, chrysocolla, arsenic, an equal amount of each; it also works against malignant ulcers. Greek (Kühn)

τῶν ἐκτὸς, ἃ Μαρκέλλας ἐπιγράφει.] Ὁ μὲν Ἀνδρόμαχος 844 ἀνέμιξε τὰ κατὰ πάθος ἁρμόττοντα ξηρὰ τοῖς κατὰ τὰ πεπονθότα. διωρισμένως δ’ ὑπ’ Ἀσκληπιάδου γέγραπται κατὰ τὸ ε΄. ἰδίαις μὲν προγραφαῖς δηλούμενα τὰ κατὰ πάθος, ἰδίαις δὲ καὶ τὰ μετὰ τόπου πεπονθότος. ἄνευ μὲν γὰρ τόπου γέγραπται πρὸς αὐτοῦ τὰ ἐπουλωτικὰ καὶ τὰ ἐσχαρωτικὰ καὶ τὰ πρὸς νομὰς ἁρμόττοντα καὶ ἄνθρακας καὶ τὰ καλούμενα ἴσχαιμα. μετὰ τόπου δὲ πεπονθότος, ὅσα πρὸς πολύποδα καὶ ὀζαίνας καὶ τὰς ἐκ μυκτήρων αἱμοῤῥαγίας καὶ τὰς ἐν στόματι νομὰς καὶ οὖλα πλαδῶντα καὶ τὰς ἐπὶ τῶν γενείων ὀχθώδεις ἐπαναστάσεις καὶ πρὸς παρωνυχίας καὶ πτερύγια καὶ πρὸς τὰς ἐν δακτυλίῳ ῥαγάδας καὶ πρὸς τὰς αἱμοῤῥοΐδας. ἔνια δὲ καὶ τὴν ὁμωνυμίαν ἔσχηκεν ἀπὸ τοῦ πεπονθότος. ἁρμόττει δὲ καὶ τοῖς ἄλλοις μέρεσιν, ὁποῖα καὶ τὰ κεφαλικὰ καλούμενα πρὸς τῶν ἰατρῶν ἐστιν, ἁρμόττοντα μὲν ἐξαιρέτως ἐν τοῖς ὀστοῖς, δυνάμενα δὲ καὶ τὰς ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ σώματι γιγνομένας ἑλκώσεις σαρκοῦν. ἐπὶ γὰρ τῆς κεφαλῆς οὐχ ὡς κεφαλῆς ἁρμόττειν μάλιστα πέφυκεν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὀχθώδεις ἐπαναστάσεις, δακτυλίῳ, Μαρκέλλας

of the External Remedies, which he entitles Marcellas.] Andromachus 844 mixed together the dry remedies that are suited according to the affection with those according to the affected parts. But by Asclepiades, in the fifth book, they have been written out separately, those according to the affection being indicated by headings of their own, and those involving an affected place likewise by headings of their own. For without a place there have been written by him the cicatrizing remedies and the escharotic ones and those suited for spreading sores (nomai) and carbuncles and the so-called haemostatic remedies (ischaima). With an affected place, however, all those for polyp and ozaenae and haemorrhages from the nostrils and spreading sores in the mouth and sodden gums and the mound-like excrescences on the chin, and for paronychiae and pterygia and for fissures in the anus and for haemorrhoids. Some, moreover, have even received their name from the affected part, yet are suited also to the other parts, such as are the remedies called 'cephalic' by physicians, which are suited pre-eminently in the case of the bones but are also able to make flesh grow in the ulcerations that arise in the whole body. For on the head it is not qua head that they are naturally best suited.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Μαρκέλλας, ὀχθώδεις ἐπαναστάσεις, πλαδῶντα, οὐχ ὡς κεφαλῆς

of the External [Remedies], which he entitles 'Marcellas'.] Andromachus 844 mixed together the dry [drugs] suited to an affection with those suited to the affected parts. But in Asclepiades they have been written separately, in the fifth [book]: those for an affection are marked by headings of their own, and those that go with an affected place likewise by headings of their own. Without reference to place he has written the cicatrizing and the escharotic [drugs], those suited to spreading ulcers and carbuncles, and the so-called haemostatics. With reference to an affected place, he has written those for polyp, ozaenae, haemorrhages from the nostrils, spreading ulcers in the mouth, sodden gums, the lumpy swellings on the chin, and those for paronychias, pterygia, fissures in the anus, and haemorrhoids. Some have also acquired their name from the affected part. Yet they suit other parts too. Such are the [drugs] called cephalic by the physicians: they are suited especially to the bones, but they are also able to incarn ulcerations arising in the whole body. For it is not as the head that they are by nature particularly suited to the head.

ἀμέλει καὶ χωρὶς τοῦ γεγυμνῶσθαι τὸ ὀστοῦν, οὐδὲν αὐτῶν χρῄζομεν, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ τῶν γεγυμνωμένων, 845 ἐπειδὴ τὴν τῶν λιπαινόντων φαρμάκων χρῆσιν εὐλαβούμεθα, τοῖς ξηροῖς μόνοις χρώμεθα, φεύγοντες τὰ καλούμενα πρὸς τῶν ἰατρῶν ἔμμοτα, διὰ μοτῶν ἐγχριομένων προσφερόμενα. καθάπερ δὲ ἐπὶ τῆς κεφαλῆς γεγυμνωμένων τῶν ὀστῶν ἡ χρῆσις αὐτῶν ἐπιτήδειός ἐστι μάλιστα, κατὰ τοῦτο κᾀπὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων ὀστῶν τῶν γεγυμνωμένων εἴτε βραχίων εἴτε πῆχυς εἴτε κερκὶς εἴτε μηρὸς εἴτε κνήμη τύχοι. διὸ κᾀγὼ τὰ ὑπ’ Ἀσκληπιάδου γεγραμμένα ξηρὰ μέλλων ἐφεξῆς γράφειν, εὑρὼν αὐτὸν ἁπάντων πρῶτα γεγραφότα τὰ κεφαλικὰ καὶ αὐτὸς γράψω πρῶτα διὰ τὸ μὴ μόνον ἐπὶ τῶν ἐν τῇ κεφαλῇ γεγυμνωμένων ὀστῶν, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων ὁμοίως πεπονθότων, ὑπάρχειν αὐτὰ χρήσιμα. δεύτερα δ’ ἐπ’ αὐτοῖς γράψω τὰ ἐπουλωτικὰ, τὰ μεταξὺ πάνθ’ ὑπερβὰς, ὅσα τόπου πεπονθότος ἐφάπτεται. μετὰ δὲ τὰ ἐπουλωτικὰ τὰ ἐφεξῆς ἅπαντα κατὰ τὴν προειρημένην ἄρτι μοι τάξιν. ἔνια μέντοι τῶν ξηρῶν τούτων φαρμάκων διττὴν ἔχει χρῆσιν, ὡς καὶ ξηροῖς αὐτοῖς δύνασθαι χρῆσθαι καὶ μιγνυμένοις ἑτέροις τισὶν, ὡς καὶ αὐτὸς Ἀσκληπιάδης ἐπισημαίνεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Indeed, when the bone has not been laid bare, we have no need of them at all; but in cases where it has been laid bare, 845 since we are wary of the use of drugs that make greasy, we use the dry ones alone, avoiding what are called by physicians emmota (lint-dressings), which are applied by means of lint-pledgets that have been smeared. And just as in the head, when the bones have been laid bare, their use is especially suitable, so in the same respect also in all the other bones that have been laid bare, whether it happens to be the upper arm or the forearm bone (ulna) or the radius or the thigh or the shin. Therefore I too, being about to write out in sequence the dry remedies recorded by Asclepiades, having found that he wrote the cephalic remedies first of all, will myself also write them first, because they are useful not only for the bones laid bare in the head, but also for all the others that are similarly affected. Second after them I shall write the cicatrizing remedies, passing over all those in between which pertain to an affected part. And after the cicatrizing remedies, all the rest in sequence according to the order I have just stated. Some of these dry drugs, however, have a twofold use, so that one can use them both dry by themselves and mixed with certain other things, as Asclepiades himself also indicates.

Indeed, when the bone has not been laid bare, we have no need of any of them; but in cases where it has been laid bare, 845 since we are wary of using greasy drugs, we use the dry ones alone, avoiding what physicians call emmota, that is, drugs applied by means of lint smeared with them. Second after them I shall write the cicatrizing drugs, passing over all those in between that concern an affected place. Some of these dry drugs, however, have a twofold use, so that one can use them both dry and mixed with certain other things, as Asclepiades himself also points out.

846 [Dry cephalic remedies.] A dry cephalic remedy which Lucius the teacher used. It detaches scales of bone, brings up corrupted bones, and fills hollows with flesh. The drug is fat-free. Rx: Illyrian iris, 4 drachms; panax, 4 drachms; aristolochia, 2 drachms; manna, 2 drachms. Others (prepare it) without aristolochia. Pound and sift everything; use it on its own and with honey. ὑπόμνημα καὶ κατὰ ταύτην τὴν πραγματείαν ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν, ἐπιδεικνύντος μου τῶν μὲν ἄνευ περιστάσεως ἁπάσης ἑλκῶν κοίλων τὰ σαρκωτικὰ φάρμακα δυνάμεως εἶναι ﻿ 848 ῥυπτικῆς ἀδήκτου. τὰ δ’ ἐπουλωτικὰ ξηραντικώτερα μετὰ τοῦ στύφειν ἀδήκτως, τὰ καθαιρετικὰ τῶν ὑπερσαρκούντων θερμὰ καὶ δριμέα. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τίνα κάλλιστα νομίζω τῶν ἐπουλωτικῶν εἶναι, προείρηταί μοι διὰ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου, καθ’ ἣν ἁπάντων παθῶν τῆς ἰάσεως αἱ μέθοδοι δελήλωνται καὶ τῶν εἰς ἕλκη διαφερουσῶν ἔμπροσθεν ἐν τῇδε τῇ νῦν προκειμένῃ. ὁ δ’ Ἀσκληπιάδης ἔγραψε φάρμακα, καθάπερ καὶ ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος ἐπουλωτικὰ, μὴ προσδιορισάμενος πότερον ἀπεριστάτων ἑλκῶν ἢ τῶν ἰδίως δυσεπουλώτων λεγομένων, ἃ διαφέρειν ἀλλήλων ἐδείχθη πλείοσι διαφοραῖς, ἴδιον ἑκάστης ἐχούσης σκοπὸν τῆς θεραπείας. ἔν τισι μέντοι προσγεγράφασιν ὅτι καὶ πρὸς κακοήθη, ἢ πάλιν, ὅτι πρὸς κονδυλώματα καὶ ῥαγάδας. ὁ δ’ Ἀσκληπιάδης καίτοι μᾶλλον τοῦ Ἀνδρομάχου τοὺς ὁρισμοὺς εἰωθὼς προστιθέναι, παρέλιπεν ἐπὶ τῶν ἐπουλωτικῶν. ἀλλ’ ἐξ ὧν ἐγὼ διῆλθον, ἔν τε τῇ θεραπευτικῇ πραγματείᾳ καὶ τῇ νῦν ἐνεστώσῃ δῆλον ἔσται τοῖς προσέχουσι τὸν νοῦν, ὅπως ἑκάστῳ τῶν γεγραμμένων χρηστέον. ξηρὰ ἐπουλωτική.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δελήλωνται

[and] a reminder also in this treatise in what precedes, where I showed that for hollow wounds without any complicating circumstance the flesh-producing drugs are of a 848 detergent, non-biting faculty, whereas the cicatrizing ones are more drying, together with astringency that does not bite, and those that reduce overgrowing flesh are hot and acrid. And indeed which of the cicatrizing ones I consider the best has been stated by me earlier in the Therapeutic Method, in which the methods of cure of all affections have been set out, and, of those pertaining to wounds, earlier in this treatise now before us. But Asclepiades wrote down cicatrizing drugs, just as Andromachus also did, without further specifying whether they are for uncomplicated wounds or for those properly called hard-to-cicatrize, which were shown to differ from one another by several differences, each having its own particular aim of treatment. In some cases, however, they have added that it is also for malignant [ulcers], or again, that it is for condylomata and fissures. But Asclepiades, although more accustomed than Andromachus to add the qualifications, omitted them in the case of the cicatrizing remedies. Yet from what I have gone through, both in the therapeutic treatise and in the one now at hand, it will be clear to those who apply their mind how each of the drugs written down is to be used. Dry cicatrizing [remedy].

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑπόμνημα (construed with the end of the preceding sentence: 'the third, fourth and fifth book'), δελήλωνται (taken as δεδήλωνται), διαφερουσῶν (taken as 'pertaining to'), ξηρὰ ἐπουλωτική (heading; noun understood)

book; and also in this treatise, in the earlier parts, where I showed that, of hollow ulcers without any complication at all, the flesh-producing drugs are of a 848 detergent, non-biting power; that the cicatrizing ones are more drying, together with being astringent without biting; and that those which remove overgrown flesh are hot and acrid. Asclepiades, however, wrote down drugs, as did Andromachus too, as cicatrizing, without further specifying whether they are for uncomplicated ulcers or for those properly called hard to cicatrize, which were shown to differ from one another by a number of differences, each having its own aim of treatment. In some cases, however, they have added in writing that it is also for malignant ones, or again, that it is for condylomata and fissures. And Asclepiades, although he is more accustomed than Andromachus to add the specifications, omitted them in the case of the cicatrizing drugs. Dry cicatrizing [powders].

The putrefactive ulcers, when they spread over the surrounding places, they call specifically nomai (spreading sores), and it is clear that such 852 conditions require the most vigorous drugs; indeed we often even use cauteries upon them. Remedies of Asclepiades for nomai. Of Lucius the teacher, for nomai: ♃ unburnt sulphur 2 dr., arsenic 2 dr., scale 2 dr., burnt copper, enough to give colour; (apply) dry and with honey. [Of Publius the teacher.] ♃ Arsenic 4 dr., scale 2 dr., elaterium 1 dr., papyrus enough to give colour. As Eunomus used it: ♃ arsenic 4 dr., scale 4 dr., unburnt sulphur 2 dr., elaterium 2 dr., burnt papyrus enough to give colour. In other copies: sandarach 4 dr., scale 6 dr., arsenic 4 dr., elaterium 2 dr., burnt papyrus 4 dr. From the writings of Areius, for nomai, which also cleanses foul ulcers without biting when dissolved in rose-oil. It works also for erosions in the womb. Theotropus gave it. ♃ arsenic 4 dr., burnt papyrus 4 dr., burnt and washed lead 4 dr., copper scale 4 dr. Triturate each separately, then, having mixed them together, use for the aforesaid conditions.

As 853 Ptolemaeus used it: ℞ burnt lead, arsenic, scale, burnt papyrus, 4 drachmas each; native sulphur, 2 drachmas; use them finely ground. As Apelles [used it]: a successful drug, entitled "the one made with papyrus", for spreading sores (nomai). It not only arrests [the spreading] but also prevents it when it is beginning. It is also effective for dysenteric patients when infused by way of rose-oil. And whenever, he says, on being infused into dysenteric patients it causes a biting sensation, the spreading sore is to be suspected. The particulars of the composition are as follows: ℞ burnt papyrus, burnt lead and burnt and washed copper, arsenic, scale of steel (stomoma), an equal amount of each; native sulphur, half the amount; use them finely ground, either with honey or with rose-oil. [Πρὸς ἄνθρακας.] Ὁ ἄνθραξ ἕλκος ἐστὶν, ἐσχάραν ταχὺ ποιοῦν, μετὰ φλεγμονῆς ἰσχυρᾶς τοῦ πέριξ μορίου παντὸς, ὥστε καὶ πυρετοὺς ἐπιφέρεσθαι σφοδροὺς 855 καὶ κινδύνους ἐσχάτους, ὥστε χρὴ κατ’ αὐτῆς μὲν τῆς ἐσχάρας ἰσχυρὸν τιθέναι φάρμακον, καὶ τούτων γε ὁποῖα πρὸς νομὰς ἁρμόζειν εἴρηται, τὰ δὲ πέριξ καταπλάσσειν καταπλάσμασιν ὁμαλὴν ἔχουσι δύναμιν, ὡς ἀποκρούεσθαί τε καὶ μετρίως ἐμψύχειν καὶ διαφορεῖν. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τῶν καταπλασμάτων ἐν τῇ περὶ τούτων πραγματείᾳ λέλεκται· νυνὶ δὲ τῶν ὑπ’ Ἀσκληπιάδου γεγραμμένων μνημονεύσομεν. πρὸς ἄνθρακας τὰ ὑπ’ Ἀσκληπιάδου. ἡ τοῦ Μασσαλιώτου. ταύτην ἐξέθηκε τῇ πόλει πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν, διὰ τὸ τοὺς ἄνθρακας ἐπιπολάζειν καὶ πολλοὺς ἀφαιρεῖσθαι. ♃ χρυσοκόλλης δραχ. δ΄. νίτρου ὀπτῆς δραχ. δ΄. λίθου Ἀσίου δραχ. δ΄. ἀρσενικοῦ δραχ. δ΄. σανδαράχης δραχ. δ΄. μίσυος ὀπτοῦ δραχ. δ΄. χαλκίτεως δραχ. δ΄. στυπτηρίας σχιστῆς δραχ. δ΄. διφρυγοῦς δραχ. δ΄. τρίψαντες καθ’ ἓν καὶ ὁμοῦ ἀποτιθέμεθα εἰς ἀγγεῖον χαλκοῦν Κύπριον. ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς χρήσεως ἐπιτίθεμεν προσθλίβοντες τῷ πλάτει τῆς μήλης, ἕως μελανθῇ ὁ ἄνθραξ, εἶτ’ ἐπιτίθεμεν ἐπάνω τὴν ὑπογεγραμμένην χλωράν. ἔστι δὲ αὕτη. ♃ λιβάνου δραχ. α΄. ἰοῦ ξυστοῦ δραχμὴν α΄.

[For anthrakes.] The anthrax is an ulcer that quickly produces an eschar, with strong phlegmone of the whole surrounding part, so that violent fevers too are brought on 855 and extreme dangers; hence one must place upon the eschar itself a strong drug, and indeed such of these as have been said to be suitable for nomai, while the surrounding parts one must plaster with cataplasms that have an even power, so as both to repel and to cool moderately and to disperse. But concerning the cataplasms it has been spoken in the treatise on these; now we shall make mention of what has been written by Asclepiades. For anthrakes, the remedies recorded by Asclepiades. The remedy of the Massaliote. He set this forth publicly for the city in view of its power, because anthrakes were prevalent and were carrying off many. ♃ chrysocolla 4 dr., roasted nitron 4 dr., Asian stone 4 dr., arsenikon 4 dr., sandarach 4 dr., roasted misy 4 dr., chalkitis 4 dr., split alum 4 dr., diphryges 4 dr. Having ground them each separately and then together, we store them in a Cyprian bronze vessel. At the time of use we apply it, pressing it on with the flat of the probe, until the anthrax turns black; then we place on top the green remedy written below. It is this: ♃ frankincense 1 dr., scraped verdigris 1 dr.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐξέθηκε τῇ πόλει πρὸς τὴν δύναμιν, ἐπιπολάζειν, ὀπτῆς (feminine with νίτρου), ὁμαλὴν

[For carbuncles.] The carbuncle is an ulcer that quickly produces an eschar, with strong inflammation of the whole surrounding part, so that violent fevers also supervene 855 and extreme dangers. Hence one must place a strong drug on the eschar itself, and indeed of these drugs such as have been said to suit spreading ulcers, and plaster the surrounding parts with poultices having an even power, so as both to repel and moderately to cool and to disperse. But concerning poultices it has been said in the treatise on them; now we shall record those written by Asclepiades. For carbuncles, those of Asclepiades. That of the Massiliote. He set this one out publicly for the city, in proportion to its power (?), because carbuncles were rife and carried off many. ♃ Chrysocolla 4 dr., roasted nitre 4 dr., Assian stone 4 dr., arsenikon 4 dr., sandarach 4 dr., roasted misy 4 dr., chalcitis 4 dr., split alum 4 dr., diphryges 4 dr. Having ground them separately and together, we store them in a Cyprian copper vessel. At the time of use we apply it, pressing it on with the flat of the probe, until the carbuncle turns black; then we put on top the green salve written below. It is this: ♃ frankincense 1 dr., scraped verdigris 1 dr.

[Ἀπίωνος πρὸς ἄνθρακας.] ♃ Νίτρου ἐρυθροῦ, ἀρσενικοῦ, σανδαράχης, χαλκίτεως, χαλκάνθου, διφρυγοῦς, ἑκάστου τὸ ἴσον, ὄξει λειοτριβῶν καὶ ξηράνας καὶ πάλιν τρίψας χρῶ. of copper scale one 856 drachm, of split alum four obols, of ammoniac salt four obols, of wax seven drachms three obols, of dry pine resin fifteen drachms, of old oil one kotyle. Grind the dry ingredients in the sun with vinegar; melt the meltable ones and pour them over, and having beaten the mixture up and taken it up, use it. In Demosthenes the Massaliote, the one who also composed the drug, it has two kotylai of oil. [Of Apion, for carbuncles.] ♃ Of red nitron, arsenic, sandarach, chalcitis, chalcanthum, diphryges, an equal amount of each; grinding fine with vinegar, drying and grinding again, use. Of Apollonius Organicus. ♃ Of chrysocolla one drachm, of split alum one drachm and of round [alum] one drachm, of sandarach one drachm, of ochre one drachm, of raw misy one drachm, of chalcanthum one drachm; use finely ground. The anthizousa ("blooming") remedy of Magnus for putrefactions, spreading sores, carbuncles, pterygia, ears flowing with pus; it also works for spreading sores in the mouth. ♃ Of split alum 8 drachms, of chalcitis 6 drachms, of sandarach 3 drachms, of dry pomegranate rinds 2 drachms, of chalcanthum 1 drachm.

857 of misy 2 drachms, of nitron 2 drachms, of dried omphacium 2 drachms. Use it dry and also with honey. Another, of Diodorus. ♃ Of omphacine oak-gall 4 drachms, of misy 2 drachms, of chalcitis 2 drachms, of arsenic 2 drachms, of sandarach 2 drachms, of split alum, chalcanthum, Asian stone, diphryges, red nitron, 1 drachm each. Sprinkle on with the ingredients finely ground, applying it with the knob of a probe. Haemostatic of Asclepiades. A well-tested drug of Lucius the teacher. ♃ Of chalcitis 16 drachms, of manna 12 drachms, of terebinth kneaded with vinegar; with these dry, sprinkle the places that are haemorrhaging. In two other copies we have rather found: ♃ of chalcitis 8 drachms, of manna 16 drachms, of roasted terebinth 16 drachms. And as Dioscorides of Tarsus gave it to Areius the Asclepiadean, checking haemorrhages marvellously: ♃ of chalcitis 8 drachms, of manna 16 drachms, of dry resin 8 drachms, of gypsum 8 drachms. The other ingredients are pounded and sifted and mixed and stored away in a bronze box; and at the time of use an equal amount of the drug is mixed with the gypsum. From the writings of Heraclides, for haemorrhoids.

τούτῳ ἐχρήσατο πρὸς 858 τὰς τῶν μυκτήρων αἱμοῤῥαγίας, ἔριον καταβάπτων καὶ συστρέφων ὥσπερ ἐλλύχνιον. ♃ σιδίων ὀξείας ῥοιᾶς οὐγγίας γ΄. στυπτηρίας Αἰγυπτίας οὐγγίας δ΄. χαλκίτεως γο α΄. λείοις χρῶ. ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν μυκτήρων ἔριον πληρώσας τοῦ φαρμάκου ἐντίθει. ὁ λεγόμενος Ἡρακλῆς πρὸς αἱμοῤῥαγίας. τούτῳ ἀντὶ καυτηρίου ἐχρήσατο, ἔστι δὲ ἐσχαρωτικόν. ποιεῖ καὶ πρὸς πτερύγια, καταστέλλει πᾶσαν ἐξοχὴν, ὀστᾶ ἀνάγει, ἀφίστησι λεπίδας. ♃ σώρεως δραχ. δ΄. χαλκάνθου δραχ. δ΄. χαλκίτεως κεκαυμένης δραχ. δ΄. ῥοὸς βυρσοδεψικοῦ δραχ. δ΄. λείοις χρῶ. ἴσχαιμον ἐπιτετευγμένον. ♃ μίσυος ξενικοῦ δραχ. στ΄. χαλκάνθου δραχ. στ΄. πίτυος φλοιοῦ δραχ. δ΄. χαλκίτεως δραχ. δ΄. μάννης δραχ. δ΄. ἄλλοι κ΄. γυψοῦ δραχ. δ΄. λείοις χρῶ. ἄλλο. ♃ λιβάνου γο α΄. μίσυος ξενικοῦ δραχ. δ΄. κηκίδων ὀμφακίνων δραχ. δ΄. χαλκίτεως δραχ. δ΄. φελλῶν κεκαυμένων δραχ. η΄. ῥοῦς βυρσοδεψικῆς δραχ. δ΄. λείοις χρῶ.

He used this for 858 haemorrhages of the nostrils, dipping wool in it and twisting it like a lamp-wick. Take pomegranate rinds of the sour pomegranate, 3 ounces; Egyptian alum, 4 ounces; chalcitis, 1 ounce; use them finely ground. In the case of the nostrils, fill a piece of wool with the drug and insert it. The so-called Heracles, for haemorrhages. He used this in place of a cautery; it is escharotic. It is also effective against pterygia, it reduces every excrescence, brings up bones, and detaches scales. Take sory, 4 dr.; chalcanthum, 4 dr.; burnt chalcitis, 4 dr.; tanners' sumach, 4 dr.; use them finely ground. A successful haemostatic. Take foreign misy, 6 dr.; chalcanthum, 6 dr.; pine bark, 4 dr.; chalcitis, 4 dr.; manna, 4 dr. (others: 20); gypsum, 4 dr.; use them finely ground. Another. Take frankincense, 1 ounce; foreign misy, 4 dr.; unripe oak-galls, 4 dr.; chalcitis, 4 dr.; burnt corks, 8 dr.; tanners' sumach, 4 dr.; use them finely ground.
